### F-EW-378 — Superior of the Carmel opposed; only Pauline encouraged at Carmel
- Claim: The Articles say that when she sought entry at 15 there was "a momentary opposition in the family", "little encouragement" at the Carmel except from Sister Agnès of Jesus, and opposition from the Carmel's ecclesiastical superior, who referred the matter to Bishop Hugonin.
- When / where: 1887, Lisieux
- People: Canon Jean-Baptiste Delatroëtte (superior; named in LAV); Mgr Hugonin
- Witness: De Teil
- Source: D3 art. 12, p. 11 (PDF 25)
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: See F-EW-379–F48 for the superior's words.
- Extraction ID: L-F02
### F-EW-379 — Canon Delatroëtte's outburst in the infirmary
- Claim: Mère Agnès testified that when the dying foundress, Mother Geneviève, at Gonzague's request, asked Canon Delatroëtte to admit Thérèse, he burst out: "Again this young girl! … one would think the salvation of the community depended on the entrance of this child … Let her remain with her father until she has reached her majority."
- When / where: 1887, infirmary of the Carmel
- People: Canon Jean-Baptiste Delatroëtte (superior); Mère Geneviève; Mère Marie de Gonzague
- Witness: Mère Agnès, Apostolic Process p. 161 (quoted by Laveille)
- Source: LAV p. 144 (PDF 164)
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: CF PDF p. 49 has a second version: Mother Geneviève asked him in the community room "in honour of this Feast" — "Oh, if you would only let little Teresa Martin enter!" — and he turned away with a refusal. Two tellings of the same request, or two requests.
- Extraction ID: L-F46
### F-EW-380 — Uncle Guérin's refusal and Delatroëtte's coldness
- Claim: Uncle Isidore Guérin at first forbade any talk of her vocation before 17, saying it would harm religion in the world's eyes, and that only a miracle would change his mind. After three days of deep desolation, he consented unexpectedly on the fourth day. Then Canon Delatroëtte, the Carmel's superior, told Pauline directly that the child was too young and would not allow entry before 21.
- When / where: autumn 1887, Lisieux
- People: Thérèse; Isidore Guérin; Canon Jean-Baptiste Delatroëtte (curé of Saint-Jacques, superior); Pauline
- Witness: Mère Agnès; HEARD Delatroëtte herself; the rest from Thérèse
- Source: PO 1, f. 145r–146r, Session 6, 13 Aug 1910
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Asked if he objected because two sisters were already there, Pauline said: "He never said that to me."
- Extraction ID: A-F34
### F-EW-381 — Sr Saint-Jean de la Croix thought Pauline imprudent to push the entry
- Claim: Opposition: A nun later admitted to Pauline that, seeing how hard Pauline worked for the entry, she had said to herself: what imprudence to let so young a child in; what imagination this Sr Agnès has; she'll be disappointed. She later admitted she had been wrong. It was Sr Saint-Jean de la Croix, dead some years by 1910.
- When / where: 1887–88 (the thought); later (the admission)
- People: Sr Saint-Jean de la Croix; Mère Agnès; Thérèse
- Witness: Mère Agnès; HEARD the admission
- Source: PO 1, f. 149v–150r, Session 6, 13 Aug 1910

- Translation: "What imprudence to let such a young child into Carmel! What an imagination that Sr Agnès has!"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high — named nun, first-hand report
- Notes: Shows opposition in the house to the Martin sisters' plans.

### F-EW-382 — Opposition: Canon Delatroëtte, superior — reasons and persistence
- Claim: Opposition: Canon Delatroëtte, curé of Saint-Jacques and ecclesiastical superior of the Carmel, would not allow entry before 21. His only stated reason to Pauline: the child was too young. He then resented steps taken over his head and the bishop's permission obtained against his view; he hesitated again over her profession and passed the decision to the bishop. His opposition lasted "several years".
- When / where: 1887–c. 1890, Lisieux
- People: Canon Jean-Baptiste Delatroëtte; Thérèse; Pauline; the bishop
- Witness: Mère Agnès; HEARD him directly

- Extraction ID: A-F156
### F-EW-383 — Liturgy: childhood devotions and feasts
- Claim: As a small child her mother had her say a morning offering of her heart to God; Thérèse repeated it by herself many times in the day. On Sunday and feast-day evenings she was sad to see the fine ceremonies end and said only heaven's happiness lasts. On Pentecost 1887 she prayed to the Apostles all day before telling her father of her vocation. Before Rome she made a pilgrimage to Notre-Dame des Victoires in Paris.
- When / where: 1870s–1887, Alençon, Lisieux, Paris
- People: Thérèse; Zélie Martin; Louis Martin
- Witness: Mère Agnès; SAW and from Thérèse
- Source: PO 1, f. 174r; f. 171r; f. 145r; f. 147r, Sessions 6 and 9
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: —
- Extraction ID: A-F182

- Claim: When Thérèse, at 14, spoke seriously of entering Carmel, Pauline was the only one who encouraged her. Marie said she was too young. Pauline says that she herself, influenced by Marie, sometimes raised objections too.

### F-EW-385 — Canon Delatroëtte's outburst in Mère Geneviève's infirmary
- Claim: On a great feast, the superior, Abbé Delatroëtte, entered the enclosure to visit the foundress, Mère Geneviève, in the infirmary. At Mère Marie de Gonzague's request, Mère Geneviève asked him before the whole community to let Thérèse enter at Christmas. He answered with emotion: was the community's salvation hanging on this child? She should stay with her father until she came of age. He said he had consulted God and did not want to hear of it again.
- When / where: autumn 1887, Lisieux Carmel infirmary
- People: Abbé Delatroëtte; Mère Geneviève de Sainte-Thérèse; Mère Marie de Gonzague; the community

- Translation: "There is no hurry. Let her stay with her father until she comes of age."

- Confidence: high — eyewitness, direct speech
- Notes: The 1910 deposition says only that he found her too young.

### F-EW-386 — Marie opposed Thérèse's vocation more strongly than Pauline
- Claim: Opposition: Marie (eldest sister, godmother, then a Carmelite) opposed Thérèse's entry to Carmel; reasons: her young age and fear of their father's grief. Marie learned about Thérèse's struggles with superiors only through what Thérèse told her in the parlour.

### F-EW-388 — The belvedere talks; the Imitation by heart
- Claim: After Marie and Pauline left, Thérèse and Céline became ever closer. Every evening at the windows of the belvedere (the top room of the Buissonnets) they shared thoughts about eternity. St John of the Cross's words "To suffer and be despised" often came to their lips. She knew the Imitation of Christ word for word; their aunt was astonished that she knew it by heart.

### F-EW-389 — No real spiritual director
- Claim: She had no director properly speaking; she saw so clearly what to do that she did not feel the need to ask. She did ask retreat preachers and extraordinary confessors at length, but rarely found the light she sought. She told novices to go to direction simply, without counting too much on help that might fail.
- When / where: 1887–97
- People: Thérèse; unnamed confessors
- Witness: Céline — first-hand
- Source: PO 4, f. 344v, 369r–v, S28, S31

- Claim: At 14 she began reading an annal of missionary nuns but soon stopped, telling Céline she did not want to know more: her desire to be a missionary was already so violent; she wanted to be a Carmelite, "to suffer more and so save more souls". ### F-EW-394 — Peaches, flower scents and a bottle of eau de Cologne
- Claim: She was not rigorist about permitted pleasures. She liked to touch fruit, especially a peach, admiring its velvet skin, and to tell flower scents apart. But if she felt a natural pleasure she stopped. At death she reproached herself, in her whole life, only with having once, for an instant, enjoyed smelling a bottle of eau de Cologne given to her on a journey.

### F-EW-410 — The glass with the triangle; Thérèse compares it to her vision of Papa
- Claim: As a girl Marie-Louise gave her cut-glass tumbler as a lamp glass before the family's Holy Face image. It blackened; cleaned, it kept a brown triangle; later it burst from too strong a flame. When her name was changed to "de la Trinité" (6 March 1896) and doubts assailed her at Compline before profession, she recalled the glass. Thérèse, moved, said this grace was of the same kind as her prophetic vision of Papa, and that her end would be like the glass: love's flame would break her body.

### F-EW-412 — Opposition: Uncle Guérin as guardian, then Canon Delatroëtte, then the prioress's delay
- Claim: Opposition: Louis agreed at once, but Uncle Isidore Guérin, as guardian, refused, postponing the plan to age 17 at least; he soon gave way. Canon Delatroëtte, ecclesiastical superior of the Carmel, refused because she was too young. The bishop gave only an evasive answer in Bayeux; Leo XIII left it to the superiors. The bishop wrote permission on 28 December 1887, but the prioress, "wishing to spare the susceptibilities of the ever-opposed superior", put off the entry until after Lent.
- When / where: May 1887 – April 1888
- People: Isidore Guérin (uncle, guardian); Canon Jean-Baptiste Delatroëtte (superior); Mgr Hugonin; Leo XIII; Mother Marie de Gonzague (prioress)

- Confidence: high
- Notes: Reasons given: age (Guérin, Delatroëtte); the prioress's reason was tactical.

- Claim: Léonie was at home on Pentecost 1887 when Thérèse told her father she wanted to enter the Carmel, but Thérèse did not tell her and Léonie noticed nothing. Léonie does not think Pauline and Marie's presence in the Carmel decided her vocation.

- Claim: Valadier's predecessor as chaplain to the condemned, Abbé Faure, assisted Pranzini at his execution (31 Aug 1887) and in 1890 told Valadier every detail. Pranzini could not have "seized" the crucifix, as the newspaper Thérèse read said, because the condemned have their arms tied behind their backs. Instead, he himself demanded the crucifix with great insistence and kissed it with passion several times.

- Claim: At about 14 she decided to convert the murderer Pranzini, confided it to Céline, begged her help, and had a Mass said. ### F-EW-419 — Opposition: Isidore Guérin's reasons
- Claim: Opposition: Uncle Isidore Guérin, consulted as the family's adviser, forbade any talk of the vocation before 17. His reasons: it was against prudence to let a 15-year-old enter Carmel; in the world's eyes it would do great harm to religion to let an inexperienced child take up such a life; only a miracle would change his mind. He gave way unexpectedly after four days.

- People: Isidore Guérin (maternal uncle, pharmacist); Thérèse

- Claim: When the sisters learned painful news at the parlour, for example about their father's health, Thérèse did not stay to find comfort in talk with her sisters. She went back at once to the community exercises. Pauline says she was far braver than Marie and Pauline in these family griefs.

### F-EW-449 — She avoided her own sisters at recreation and sought those less friendly to her
- Claim: Thérèse did not seek the company of her blood sisters. At recreation she chose nuns who were less sympathetic to her, and often those who were alone or neglected.

### F-EW-450 — Three directors: one left for Canada, one called her proud, one gave peace
- Claim: Opposition: In Carmel she searched for years for a director. The first barely heard her and left for Canada, writing a few lines once a year. Another, surprised by her aim at very high holiness, told her it was pride to want to equal or surpass St Teresa. A third, in 1891, assured her she did not offend God and could follow her way of trust.
- When / where: 1888–1891
- People: Thérèse; Fr Pichon (the Canada director, not named here); an unnamed priest; Fr Alexis (1891)

### F-EW-451 — Clothing and profession were both delayed
- Claim: She could have taken the habit by the end of October 1888, but it was delayed to 10 Jan 1889 in the hope that her sick father could attend. She could have made profession on 11 Jan 1890, but the prioress, expecting the superior to object because of her age, told her to wait; Pauline was present and agreed with the refusal. The superior, still hesitating, referred the vote to the bishop; she made profession on 8 Sept 1890.
- When / where: 1888–1890, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Mère Marie de Gonzague; Mère Agnès; Canon Delatroëtte; the bishop
- Witness: Mère Agnès; SAW (present at the refusal)
- Source: PO 1, f. 150r–151r, Session 6, 13 Aug 1910
- Original words: « J'étais moi-même avec notre révérende mère prieure, lorsqu'elle lui fit ce refus, auquel je m'associai moi-même. »
- Translation: "I was with our reverend mother prioress when she gave her this refusal, and I joined in it myself."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Thérèse felt "deep sorrow", then accepted it in prayer.
- Extraction ID: A-F39
### F-EW-452 — She never complained of cold, and never rubbed her hands
- Claim: Only near the end did the sisters learn that cold made her suffer very much. Yet she was never seen rubbing her hands in winter or taking any posture that betrayed it. She never said "it's very cold" or "it's hot". Her requests for extra penances were refused because of her delicate constitution.
- When / where: 1888–1897, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse
- Witness: Mère Agnès; SAW

### F-EW-453 — Eating was "shameful"; the refectory was a torment; she was given leftovers
- Claim: In 1897 she told Pauline that as a child she found eating a shameful function that should be hidden or kept in the family. In Carmel she went to the refectory "as to a torture". Because she never complained, the sisters came to think she had no dislikes, and readily served her the leftovers of earlier meals, which she always accepted.

### F-EW-462 — Her work assignments, year by year
- Claim: From entry: linen room for nine months; after her clothing, refectory for two years; then sacristy until June 1892. From then to February 1893: painting work (the oratory fresco, altar ornaments, images sold outside). In that period she was also named "tierce" (third companion) of the bursar and went with her whenever workmen came in. At the 1893 elections she was named portress (tourière assistant inside) while still painting. In March 1896 she went back to the sacristy. After she fell really ill she was taken off it and got leave to help a mentally ill sister in the linen room until her strength gave out. She took to bed in the infirmary on 8 July 1897.
- When / where: 1888–1897
- People: Thérèse
- Witness: Mère Agnès
- Source: PA 6, [371]–[372], S12 6 Jul 1915
- New to English readers: yes (the precise sequence and the bursar role)
- Confidence: high — she was in the house and prioress part of the time
- Notes: 1910 only lists offices. On the March 1896 date see Gaps (hemoptysis).
- Extraction ID: B-F47

- Claim: She very much wanted to be infirmarian, because of the many chances for charity there, and to hear at the Judgment "I was sick and you visited me". She never got it.

- Claim: Sr Marie-Philomène made some months of novitiate with Thérèse. Pauline calls her "very holy but also very limited", and humble enough to admit it. ### F-EW-467 — Early in Carmel she made her food tasteless; she gave it up
- Claim: She admitted that early in religious life she tried, like the saints, to make her food tasteless. She dropped this long ago: when food was to her taste she blessed God, and when it was bad she accepted it. She found unsought mortification the safest. She thought one should enjoy nature and music when moved by thanks to God.

### F-EW-468 — No preference for her sisters; she came to Pauline the least
- Claim: In Carmel she showed no marked preference for her three sisters; at recreation she never sought them nor avoided them, and often talked with a sister who was alone. Under Mère de Gonzague she never confided her struggles to Pauline, though she could have got leave. Mère de Gonzague had set weekly talks with the prioress (instead of monthly as in the Constitutions); when Pauline became prioress in 1893 the sisters kept coming weekly, but Thérèse thought that would be seeking herself, and Pauline saw her least of all.
- When / where: 1888–1896

### F-EW-469 — The night-light for the prioress's relatives: "the devil tempted me to revolt"
- Claim: She was asked to prepare a night-light for Mère de Gonzague's sister and little grand-nephew, who, against custom, often stayed in the outside turn-sisters' building. She told Pauline the devil tempted her violently to revolt, not only against the lamp that wasted her time but against the prioress, who put part of the community at her family's service and allowed her own relatives what she would never allow other sisters' families. She asked God to calm the storm and prepared the lamp as if it were for the Virgin and the Child Jesus, with not a grain of dust. Her heart grew calm, and she was ready to serve the prioress's relatives all night. From that day she resolved never to judge whether orders were useful.
- When / where: undated (Gonzague prioress: 1888–1893 or 1896–1897)
- People: Thérèse; Mère Marie de Gonzague; her sister and grand-nephew
- Witness: Mère Agnès; HEARD Thérèse
- Source: PA 6, [466]–[468], S17 13 Jul 1915; [479], S18
- Original words: « Le diable … me tentait violemment de révolte … contre les agissements de Notre Mère »
- Translation: "The devil … tempted me violently to revolt … against Our Mother's doings"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Absent in 1910. One of the few places where Thérèse herself criticizes the prioress.

### F-EW-470 — Poverty: loved beautiful things, chose the ugliest, mended to the end
- Claim: She told Pauline that at first she studied the vow of poverty closely because she naturally loved beautiful things and wanted to lack nothing, so it would cost her. She let others take even needed tools from her work without asking them back. She chose the ugliest and least handy objects. She mended her clothes until they were worn out. She never lost a moment; told not to tire herself, she said poverty obliged her to work.

- People: Thérèse

### F-EW-484 — Opposition: community — the novice "badly cared for" (parlour reports 1888–94)
- Claim: Opposition: Through weekly parlour visits Céline learned the young novice suffered much. Pauline grieved to see her badly cared for, contradicted by several sisters, "scolded rightly and wrongly". Thérèse, pale but joyful, said she was not unhappy and had enough to live on. Causes given: near-constant dryness in prayer; some nuns who abused her patience, passing her all the leftovers — several times only a few herring heads or scraps reheated for days; and the "rather defective" government of Mère Marie de Gonzague.
- When / where: 1888–94, Lisieux Carmel parlour
- People: Mère Agnès (source of the complaints); unnamed nuns; Mère Marie de Gonzague
- Witness: Céline — HEARD from Pauline and Thérèse in the parlour; confirmed by nuns after 1894
- Source: PO 4, f. 348v–349r, S29, 19 Sept 1910
- Original words: « elle n'eût dans son assiette que quelques têtes de hareng »
- Translation: "she had on her plate only a few herring heads"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium — second-hand, from Pauline, who was not neutral
- Notes: Matches Marie (F-EW-480).
- Extraction ID: C-F110
### F-EW-485 — Opposition: Gonzague's "unstable and bizarre" rule
- Claim: Opposition: Céline says Mère Marie de Gonzague's "unstable and bizarre" character made the nuns suffer much. Everything depended on the whim of the moment; a good arrangement lasted little; only by diplomacy and cleverness could one enjoy a tolerable situation for a few weeks.
- When / where: 1888–97, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague (prioress)
- Witness: Céline — heard 1888–94, SAW 1894–97
- Source: PO 4, f. 349r, S29
- Original words: « dont le caractère instable et bizarre faisait beaucoup souffrir les religieuses »
- Translation: "whose unstable and odd character made the nuns suffer greatly"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high (as sworn opinion)
- Notes: Stronger than Marie's wording (F-EW-478).
- Extraction ID: C-F111
### F-EW-486 — "Wholesale trade": souls through priests
- Claim: Entering Carmel her special aim was to pray for priests. She called this "doing business wholesale", since by reaching the head she reached the members. At her canonical examination before profession she said she came to save souls and above all to pray for priests. Céline notes each candidate answers what she wishes; this answer was her own.
- When / where: 1888–90, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; the examining superior (unnamed)
- Witness: Céline — HEARD from Thérèse
- Source: PO 4, f. 344r–v, S28
- Original words: « faire le commerce en gros, puisque par la tête elle atteignait les membres »
- Translation: "doing business wholesale, since through the head she reached the members"
- New to English readers: yes (the phrase)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Distinctive per Céline.
- Extraction ID: C-F121
### F-EW-487 — Opposition: a novice mistress "without discernment" and a scolding prioress
- Claim: Opposition: Her early formation was marked by "the severity or incapacity" of those in charge. She was poorly fed and rested and treated severely by her prioress. Her novice mistress was a holy nun "but without discernment": after forgetting her for weeks, she would suddenly make her rest for fifteen days in a row for no reason; then the prioress, not seeing her at morning prayer, would lose her temper and scold her. The poor child did not know whom to obey.
- When / where: 1888–90, Lisieux Carmel
- People: novice mistress (Sr Marie des Anges, not named here); prioress (Mère Marie de Gonzague)
- Witness: Céline — heard (she was not yet in Carmel)
- Source: PO 4, f. 379r–v, S32, 22 Sept 1910
- Original words: « une sainte religieuse, mais sans discernement »
- Translation: "a holy nun, but lacking discernment"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium — hearsay, but detailed
- Notes: Important critical picture of the novitiate.
- Extraction ID: C-F129

- Claim: At the start of his illness, Céline noticed that her father constantly wanted to veil his face, taking his handkerchief or any cloth to hand and putting it on his head — matching Thérèse's childhood vision.
- When / where: 1888–89
- People: Louis Martin
- Witness: Céline — SAW

### F-EW-489 — She never asked for news
- Claim: She never asked for news. If the prioress seemed to be telling something interesting to a group, she kept away.
- When / where: 1888–97
- People: Thérèse; the prioress

### F-EW-490 — Posture: never leaned back, never crossed her feet, did not wipe sweat
- Claim: She never leaned her back, never crossed her feet, always sat straight, and disliked anyone sitting crookedly even to rest. Unless really necessary she did not wipe off sweat, saying it admitted one was too hot and made it known.
- When / where: 1888–97, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse
- Witness: Céline — SAW
- Source: PO 4, f. 382r, S32
- Original words: « c'était convenir qu'on avait trop chaud et une manière de le faire savoir »
- Translation: "it was admitting you were too hot and a way of letting it be known"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: —
- Extraction ID: C-F177
### F-EW-491 — The discipline: "as much harm as possible", tears, a smile
- Claim: Céline said instinct makes one stiffen under the discipline to hurt less. Thérèse looked at her stunned: she took the discipline to hurt herself and wanted it to hurt as much as possible. Sometimes it hurt so much tears came, but she tried to smile. She said her devotion would be to wear an instrument of penance every day that was not a discipline day, and did so as long as it was permitted.
- When / where: 1888–96, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Céline
- Witness: Céline — HEARD
- Source: PO 4, f. 382r–v, S32
- Original words: « je prends la discipline pour me faire du mal et je veux qu'elle me fasse le plus de mal possible »
- Translation: "I take the discipline to hurt myself, and I want it to hurt me as much as possible"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: —
- Extraction ID: C-F178
### F-EW-492 — Poorer and uglier: the velvet, the pearl pin, the lamp, the dresses
- Claim: When a sister edged her fraying workbasket with old velvet from the attic, Thérèse, though very busy, undid it and sewed the velvet on inside out to make it poorer and uglier. In the linen room her companion gave her a pin with a pearl-like head; she broke off the white head. She made a novice scrub off linseed oil the novice had put on her cell furniture. All her religious life she used a small lamp whose mechanism was broken, raising and lowering the wick with a pin. She did not care how her habits fitted; the uglier the better.
- When / where: 1888–97, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; unnamed sisters and novice
- Witness: Céline — SAW
- Source: PO 4, f. 385v–386r, S32
- Original words: « remit le velours à l'envers, pour que ce fût plus pauvre et plus laid »
- Translation: "put the velvet back on inside out, so it would be poorer and uglier"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Céline admits they should have realised she would have liked a lamp like the others'.
- Extraction ID: C-F191
### F-EW-493 — Mother Marie de Gonzague was cold to the postulant
- Claim: Opposition: Marie says the prioress, Mother Marie de Gonzague, showed little sympathy for the young postulant, often scolded her, or paid her no attention. The prioress was used to being flattered by everyone; Thérèse did not seek her favour that way, so she went unnoticed, or received only coldness.
- When / where: Lisieux Carmel, from 1888
- People: Mother Marie de Gonzague, prioress; Thérèse
- Witness: Marie, saw it (in the community)
- Source: PA 7, [568], Session 22
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: medium-high — Marie's judgment, consistent with Geneviève
- Notes: Marie also lists "almost constant dryness" as a hidden trial.
- Extraction ID: D-F26
### F-EW-494 — She stopped mid-word at the bell
- Claim: At the first sound of the bell she stopped writing, even in the middle of a word. Marie kept a note ending "I must leave you, 9 o'clock is strik..." When she saw Marie finish a line after the bell, she said it would be far better to lose it and make an act of regularity.
- When / where: Carmel, 1888 onward
- People: Thérèse; Marie
- Witness: Marie, saw it; kept the note (LT 49)
- Source: PA 7, [600], Session 25
- Original words: « Je suis obligée de vous quitter, 9 heures son... »
- Translation: "I have to leave you, 9 o'clock is ring..."

- Confidence: high
- Notes: —
- Extraction ID: D-F70
### F-EW-495 — She sat each day beside a teasing lay-sister novice
- Claim: Opposition: As a postulant, at recreation she managed to sit beside a young lay sister, her companion in the novitiate, who enjoyed teasing her in bad taste. She listened humbly to all her nonsense and, instead of avoiding her, sat beside her every day.
- When / where: Carmel, 1888
- People: Thérèse; unnamed young lay-sister novice
- Witness: Marie, saw it
- Source: PA 7, [600], Session 25

### F-EW-496 — In life, few knew her; Gonzague said there was none like her
- Claim: In life she passed unnoticed; apart from her sisters and some novices, few knew her. Yet Mother Marie de Gonzague, while sometimes jealous of her, said there was none like her in the community. Others noticed her for obeying the prioress's smallest recommendations. A lay sister who had humiliated her unjustly judged her a saint after seeing her virtue then.
- When / where: Carmel, 1888–97
- People: Mother Marie de Gonzague; unnamed lay sister
- Witness: Marie
- Source: PA 7, [620], Session 26

### F-EW-497 — Opposition: habit and profession delayed "to humour the superior"
- Claim: Opposition: She should have taken the habit six months after entry, in October 1888, but it was put off to 10 January 1889 because of her youth, "always to humour the superior". She could canonically have made profession on 11 January 1890, but it was delayed again "on the pretext of her youth", though she was 17; she made vows on 8 September 1890.
- When / where: Carmel, 1888–90
- People: Canon Delatroëtte; the prioress
- Witness: Céline, hearsay (she was not yet in Carmel)
- Source: PA 8, [647], Session 28, 28 July 1915
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The text misprints "1989".
- Extraction ID: D-F128
### F-EW-498 — Her jobs: sacristy, linen room, never infirmarian
- Claim: In 1896 she was in charge of the sacristy, a job she had held before Céline's entry. At the start of her illness she became aide in the linen room, and finally was freed of all jobs when her strength failed. She was indifferent to all charges, but would have much liked to be infirmarian to exercise charity; she was never appointed.
- When / where: Carmel, 1888–97
- People: Thérèse
- Witness: Céline
- Source: PA 8, [648]–[649], Session 28
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: At [782] Céline says she was put in the linen room at her own request, under Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph.
- Extraction ID: D-F130
### F-EW-499 — Opposition: three Martin sisters in one house caused jealousy
- Claim: Opposition: The postulant went unnoticed at first. Then her arrival, making three sisters of one family in the community, raised "certain jealousies" in the community. These did not target her personally, but she suffered from them. Céline heard that in her novitiate years she was often tested by the prioress's severity.
- When / where: Carmel, 1888–90
- People: the community; Mother Marie de Gonzague
- Witness: Céline, hearsay
- Source: PA 8, [649], Session 28
- New to English readers: partial

- Notes: Reason given: family bloc in one convent.

### F-EW-500 — Petals for the crucifix, not for people
- Claim: Her devotion to the Passion dated from age five, when she first understood a sermon on it. In Carmel she threw flowers at the crucifix in the courtyard; in her illness she covered her crucifix with rose petals, choosing the freshest. She would not give creatures this sign: once, when flowers were put in her hand to throw at someone as a sign of affection, she refused.
- When / where: Carmel, 1888–97
- People: Thérèse
- Witness: Céline, saw it
- Source: PA 8, [656]–[657], Session 28
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: —
- Extraction ID: D-F142
### F-EW-501 — Patrons and "friends" among the saints
- Claim: Her protectors: her patron saints Teresa, Francis de Sales and Martin, and in Carmel John of the Cross. Her chosen "friends": St Cecilia ("the saint of abandonment"), Blessed Joan of Arc, Blessed Théophane Vénard and the Holy Innocents. She loved Vénard as a "simple little saint" who loved Mary and his family. She received his portrait and relics in her last illness and they never left her. She wrote a hymn on the responses of St Agnes.
- When / where: Carmel, 1888–97
- People: Thérèse
- Witness: Céline
- Source: PA 8, [669]–[670], Session 29
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Liturgy. She also said the saints who seem deaf are loved more.
- Extraction ID: D-F159
### F-EW-502 — Parlour talks finished by Jesus
- Claim: When Céline, still in the world, left the parlour sad because time ran out, Thérèse asked Jesus to tell Céline what she had meant to say. At the next parlour Céline reported good inspirations she had had, which turned out to be exactly the advice Thérèse had wanted to give.
- When / where: Carmel parlour, 1888–94
- People: Thérèse; Céline
- Witness: Céline, involved
- Source: PA 8, [682]–[683], Session 30
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium — Céline's interpretation
- Notes: See M-EW-015.

### F-EW-503 — Hidden medals in workmen's clothes
- Claim: In Carmel she slipped medals of the Virgin into the clothes of workmen, hiding them carefully in the linings.

- People: Thérèse; workmen at the Carmel

### F-EW-504 — Masses for Pranzini paid with birthday money
- Claim: She had made the "Heroic Act", giving all her merits and all future suffrages to the Virgin. The only suffrages she applied to a special intention were for Pranzini. Whenever the family offered her a present for a feast or birthday, she asked for money, and with the prioress's permission had Masses said for his soul: "He is my child; I must not forget him now."
- When / where: Carmel, 1888–97
- People: Thérèse; Henri Pranzini (executed 31 August 1887); the family; the prioress
- Witness: Céline
- Source: PA 8, [712]–[713], Session 32
- Original words: « C'est mon enfant, il ne faut pas que je l'oublie maintenant »
- Translation: "He's my child; I mustn't forget him now."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: On her profession day she asked God to empty purgatory.
- Extraction ID: D-F210
### F-EW-505 — Opposition: the "troubled government" of Mother Marie de Gonzague
- Claim: Opposition: Her whole religious life passed under the "troubled government" of Mother Marie de Gonzague, who, prioress or not, would not tolerate anyone else holding authority. "One cannot imagine the diplomacy needed to avoid scenes." Thérèse saw all the prioress's faults, deplored them, and tried to stop the harm to the community, while keeping full deference.
- When / where: Carmel, 1888–97
- People: Mother Marie de Gonzague; Thérèse
- Witness: Céline, from 1894
- Source: PA 8, [719], Session 33

- Translation: "You cannot imagine the diplomacy it took to avoid scenes."
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium — strong, one-sided view; editors urge caution
- Notes: Reasons given: Gonzague's need to hold authority, and jealousy.
- Extraction ID: D-F215
### F-EW-506 — Flowers from Céline left to wilt at the turn
- Claim: When Céline, still in the world, sent flowers, Thérèse received them directly as portress (at the turn) but would not keep them for her little Jesus without an express order from the prioress; she would sooner let them wilt. In her illness, when relatives sent fruit, she said the grapes were good, "but I don't like what comes to me from my family".
- When / where: Carmel turn, 1888–94; infirmary 1897
- People: Thérèse; Céline; the Guérin relatives
- Witness: Céline
- Source: PA 8, [730]–[731], Session 34, 26 August 1915

- Translation: "How good these grapes are! But I don't like what comes to me from my family."

- Confidence: high
- Notes: Job: portress / turn aide.
- Extraction ID: D-F222
### F-EW-507 — Eating should be hidden
- Claim: In the world she could not understand people inviting each other to dinner; she said one should hide and do this "so low" act almost in secret, and was consoled only by thinking that Our Lord submitted to our needs.
- When / where: Buissonnets, before 1888
- People: Thérèse
- Witness: Céline
- Source: PA 8, [735], Session 34

### F-EW-508 — Opposition: "frightful scenes" under Gonzague, always from jealousy
- Claim: Opposition: She lived always under Mother Marie de Gonzague's tutelage. Céline says everything was at the whim of the moment; rules were made and unmade; "frightful scenes broke out like a storm over nothing, but always jealousy was the cause". Such rule was a constant temptation to murmur; Thérèse bore gently the evil she could not prevent and allowed no bitter criticism of the prioress.

- People: Mother Marie de Gonzague; Thérèse; the community

### F-EW-509 — The lamp raised with a pin; pens softened in milk
- Claim: All her religious life she had a little lamp whose wick could be raised only by pulling it with a pin. When Céline entered, Thérèse gave her own decent writing case and holy-water stoup and found for herself discarded ones in the attics. She used pens to the last; in her illness she dipped them in milk "to make them soft again".

- People: Thérèse; Céline

### F-EW-510 — Communities did not have daily communion
- Claim: Thérèse envied those who received communion daily, because the community did not have that privilege then. Her greatest suffering was not being able to receive every day.
- When / where: 1888–1897
- People: Thérèse
- Witness: MT
- Source: PO 17, f. 1069v; PA 21, p. 1228

- Confidence: high

### F-EW-511 — Ordered to report every stomach ache
- Claim: Opposition: As postulant and novice, Thérèse's mistress (PA names Sr Marie des Anges) ordered her to report every stomach ache. She had one every day, and reporting it was torture: she would have preferred "a hundred blows with a stick". The mistress forgot the order and said "you will never have the health to keep the Rule". Or she asked Gonzague for a remedy, and Gonzague answered crossly: "this child is always complaining… if she can't bear her ills, let her leave!" Thérèse went on reporting at the risk of being sent away, until she was freed from the order.
- When / where: 1888–1890, novitiate
- People: Thérèse; Sr Marie des Anges (novice mistress); Mère Marie de Gonzague
- Witness: MT, heard it from Thérèse
- Source: PO 17, f. 1086v; PA 21, p. 1268
- Original words: « Mais enfin, cette enfant-là se plaint toujours! On vient au Carmel pour souffrir; si elle ne peut pas porter ses maux, qu'elle s'en aille! »
- Translation: Really, that child is always complaining! One comes to Carmel to suffer; if she can't bear her ills, let her go!
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high — second-hand from Thérèse, told consistently twice
- Notes: Daily stomach pain in 1888–1890 is a notable health fact. See E-Q05.
- Extraction ID: E-F69
### F-EW-512 — Opposition: jealousy against "the four Martin sisters"
- Claim: Opposition: Thérèse passed almost unnoticed; only four or five nuns, MT among them, saw her perfection. Most thought her a very regular nun with nothing to reproach. But many nuns felt jealous of the group "of the four Martin sisters", a phrase used "disdainfully" (PA). Even those hostile to the group made an exception for Thérèse. MT says the antipathy was stirred and fed by Gonzague.
- When / where: 1888–1897, Lisieux
- People: the Martin sisters; unnamed nuns; Mère Marie de Gonzague
- Witness: MT, observation
- Source: PO 17, f. 1094v–1095r, Session 66; PA 21, p. 1285–1286, Session 65
- Original words: « ce groupe des quatre sœurs Martin, comme elles appelaient dédaigneusement sœur Thérèse de l'Enfant Jésus et ses sœurs »

- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Reason for the opposition: resentment at four sisters of one family in one Carmel (a departure from the rules, which Gonzague had obtained from the bishop).
- Extraction ID: E-F117
### F-EW-513 — Opposition: "two or three bad spirits" who changed after her death
- Claim: Opposition: MT says Gonzague's admiration was the private view of the whole community, "apart from two or three bad spirits" who are no longer in the community, and who changed their minds after Thérèse's death.

### F-EW-514 — Never refused a service
- Claim: A sister could disturb Thérèse at any time and be well received; she never showed annoyance. When she could not grant a request, she excused herself so kindly that the sister left as pleased as if she had. She told MJ: never say no; see God in each sister.
- When / where: 1888–1897
- People: Thérèse; MJ
- Witness: MJ
- Source: PO 15, f. 621r–v; PA 18, p. 1063–1064
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high

### F-EW-515 — Marthe's jealousy and sulks
- Claim: MJ admits she made Thérèse suffer by her difficult character. She loved her but sometimes felt jealous, and when corrected got angry, went away and refused to speak. Once, cross, she said hurtful things; Thérèse calmly begged her help with a job. MJ helped grumbling, then tested her patience by refusing to answer. She could not break her gentleness and finally asked pardon. In PA: "another sister in her place would have given me up, so unbearable was I".
- When / where: undated, 1888–1897
- People: MJ; Thérèse
- Witness: MJ, her own conduct

- Translation: another sister in her place would have abandoned me, I was so unbearable
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: See E-S14. MJ wondered what Thérèse could see in "a poor little lay sister".

### F-EW-516 — She never leaned back
- Claim: Out of mortification Thérèse never leaned back when seated, even when most tired and could barely drag herself along.

### F-EW-517 — Leftovers and hidden tastes
- Claim: In the kitchen, not knowing to whom to give the leftovers, they always served them to Thérèse, knowing nothing would come back. So her tastes were hard to know. Only in her last illness did she admit that certain foods made her ill each time she ate them.
- When / where: 1888–1897, kitchen and refectory
- People: Thérèse; the kitchen sisters (MJ among them)
- Witness: MJ, kitchen sister

### F-EW-518 — Eyes down, never running, passing groups by
- Claim: Thérèse walked with modesty and recollection, eyes always lowered, never running, not trying to see or know what went on. She gave no opinion unless asked. When she saw sisters talking in a group she went straight past and told the novices to do the same.
- When / where: 1888–1897
- People: Thérèse
- Witness: MJ
- Source: PO 15, f. 627r; PA 18, p. 1066–1067

### F-EW-519 — At the bell, not even a stitch more; the angel's blessing
- Claim: At the first sound of the bell Thérèse left everything, even in a conversation, and put down her needle without finishing the stitch. So she was always first in choir, which made her happy because there, she said, she received the blessing of the community's angel.
- When / where: 1888–1897
- People: Thérèse
- Witness: MJ; MM also saw her leave all at the first bell

- Claim: MJ never saw her waste a moment; she was "a miser with her time". She told MJ to be scrupulous about it, "because time does not belong to us". She also picked up a match or a scrap of paper out of poverty.

- Claim: MJ says the nuns certainly saw Thérèse as very fervent, but her simplicity and humility hid her heroism. Only the novices who were close to her saw her as a saint. Since her death all venerate her, even those who did not notice her holiness.

### F-EW-525 — Detached from her sisters — except once
- Claim: Aimée saw that Thérèse's own sisters paid her great attention, while she was perfectly detached from these family ties. Only once did she seem strongly affected by a sorrow that befell one of her sisters; Aimée calls this "barely an imperfection", perhaps not one in God's eyes. She never saw her fail in charity, even in words.
- When / where: 1888–1897
- People: Thérèse; the Martin sisters
- Witness: AJ, observation
- Source: PA 17, p. 1045, Session 55
- Original words: « une fois seulement elle m'a paru vivement affectée d'une peine qui était arrivée à une de ses sœurs; mais c'est à peine une imperfection »
- Translation: only once did she seem to me deeply affected by a sorrow that had befallen one of her sisters; but that is hardly an imperfection
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The only "fault" any witness in this batch names. The remark that her sisters "had great attentions for her" hints at the favouritism critics alleged. See E-Q23.
- Extraction ID: E-F218
### F-EW-526 — The witness is the nun Thérèse found antipathetic (editors' note)
- Claim: The modern editors state that Thérèse felt a strong natural antipathy for this witness. By her constant smiling kindness she made the nun believe the opposite. The unnamed sister of Manuscript C, fol. 13v–14r, is this witness.
- When / where: 1888–1897, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Sr Thérèse de Saint-Augustin

- Confidence: high — the editors state it plainly; the identification is traditional and also rests on TSA's own "Souvenirs"
- Notes: The witness herself never shows awareness of this. She says she had "a certain intimacy" with Thérèse (PO [577v]) and that "our souls understood each other" (PA [814]). The irony is useful for a writer. See also F-F155 (MdA's account of the same antipathy) and F-EW-2291.
- Extraction ID: F-F01

- Claim: TSA says she lived near Thérèse "in a certain intimacy" from 1888 to 1897. She says she will use almost nothing from Story of a Soul or from other sisters. In 1915 she adds that "our souls understood each other."

### F-EW-528 — The refectory neighbour who took nearly everything
- Claim: As a postulant and for part of her religious life, Thérèse sat in the refectory next to a sister who took almost everything for herself. Thérèse never remarked on it and went without necessities. Much later she had to speak of it because of her job and out of charity for that sister.
- When / where: from 1888, refectory, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; an unnamed sister
- Witness: TSA, observed
- Source: PO-13 [585r], session 55; PA-09 [836], session 41
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium
- Notes: In 1915 TSA softens it: the neighbour acted "no doubt through absent-mindedness," and Thérèse waited for Providence. The 1911 detail that Thérèse finally spoke up is dropped.
- Extraction ID: F-F28
### F-EW-529 — Community opinions: cold and proud, and jealousy of the four Martins
- Claim: Opposition: TSA heard differing opinions of Thérèse in her lifetime. Those who knew her best, especially her novices, admired her. For others she passed unnoticed because of her simplicity. Some judged her unfavourably: some accused her of coldness and pride. TSA thinks this was because she spoke little and stayed reserved, and perhaps because the presence of four sisters in the community stirred opposition or jealousy. She says the surviving opponents have all changed their minds.
- Opposition detail: Unnamed sisters. Type: accusation of coldness and pride; jealousy. Documented reasons (TSA's opinion): she spoke little and was reserved; four Martin sisters in one community stirred opposition or jealousy.
- When / where: 1888–1897, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; unnamed sisters
- Witness: TSA, HEARD these opinions
- Source: PO-13 [585v–586r], session 55, 14 Feb 1911
- Original words: « quelques-unes l'accusaient de froideur et de fierté »
- Translation: some accused her of coldness and pride.
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high — a sworn statement against interest
- Notes: CONFLICT with MdA, who said in 1911 she knew of no one with a different opinion (F-EW-1932). In 1915 TSA says only "a small number" failed to see her virtue (PA [846–847]).
- Extraction ID: F-F31

- People: TSA; Thérèse

### F-EW-531 — Severe treatment by the prioress from the postulancy
- Claim: Opposition: As a postulant Thérèse was treated very severely by the Mother Prioress. TSA never saw her surrounded with care or spared. This did not change over the years.

### F-EW-532 — Editors: the novice mistress was absent-minded and hurt Thérèse unwittingly
- Claim: The editors say MdA was rather absent-minded and easily forgot what she had said, and so was often an involuntary cause of suffering to Thérèse. Thérèse had great difficulty opening her soul to her, but later managed and was consoled. MdA worked beside Thérèse in the linen room when she began, and wrote her seven short notes (Oct 1888–Oct 1890). Her availability to others was "perhaps pushed to excess."

### F-EW-533 — Running into her novice mistress's arms, heartbroken by the prioress
- Claim: Opposition: As a novice Thérèse would run "with a child's simplicity" to tell MdA the trials she met from the prioress, who at times was severe with her. Thérèse could see in it only "a human feeling," but kept this impression to herself. MdA "still sees" her one day rushing to throw herself into her arms to confide her broken heart, without a murmur, and smiling despite all.
- Opposition detail: Mother Marie de Gonzague, prioress. Type: severity toward a novice. Documented reason: none; Thérèse could see in it only 'a human feeling.' MdA (PA [870]) says God permitted it to keep Thérèse from a human attachment to the prioress.

- Claim: Thérèse told MdA about her spiritual desolations cheerfully, accepting them so that God would give consolations to souls instead.

### F-EW-535 — She suffered from cold "to the point of dying" and never said so
- Claim: Cold was extremely hard for Thérèse, but she never told MdA. MdA learned only recently that she suffered so much "that it was enough to die of." MdA exclaims: "Ah, if I had known! What would I not have done to remedy it!"

### F-EW-536 — Leftovers and food a strong stomach could hardly bear
- Claim: MdA often had a heavy heart to see this young, frail child denied the dietary relief she needed. "Many times" she was served only leftovers or food that a strong stomach would hardly bear. The same was true for rest and sleep. Thérèse never said a word.

### F-EW-537 — An hour's direction with the prioress, scolded nearly all the time
- Claim: Opposition: Several sisters wrongly thought Thérèse was spoiled in every way. In fact the prioress tested her hard. When they met, she got only reproaches. In her direction sessions she stayed with the prioress an hour and was scolded "almost the whole time." What hurt most was not understanding how to correct the faults she was blamed for.

### F-EW-538 — Weeding at 4:30 and "This child does absolutely nothing"
- Claim: Opposition: MdA sent the postulant daily at 4:30 to pull weeds in the garden for fresh air. Thérèse found it hard but said nothing, and it was an occasion to meet the prioress, who humiliated her: "What sort of novice has to be sent for a walk every day?" She also heard: "This child does absolutely nothing." Later Thérèse thanked the prioress for this "precious" training.
- Opposition detail: Mother Marie de Gonzague, prioress. Type: humiliating remarks. Documented reason: the daily garden walk; alleged lack of work and of devotion in her jobs.

- Claim: Thérèse, with her "remarkable finesse of mind," saw the gaps that went with the fine qualities of Mother Marie de Gonzague, "this mother whom we loved in spite of everything." Knowing what made her suffer, Thérèse wrapped her in tenderness with childlike ways, consoled and enlightened her. ### F-EW-543 — Poverty: the chipped jug and the lamp
- Claim: As a postulant she liked neat things and having what she needed to hand. Gradually she gave this up: her pretty cell jug was replaced by a coarse chipped one; she came to love the ugliest, least handy objects. One evening her lamp was taken by mistake when she had much work; she was tempted to impatience, then rejoiced.

- People: Thérèse

- Claim: Abbé Youf confessed her from entry to death and esteemed her highly. Abbé Baillon, said to be one of the most learned priests of the diocese, whom she liked to consult, had great regard for her. Père Armand Lemonnier thought her a predestined soul, called her "the little flower," and valued her advice.

### F-EW-545 — The sand-glass: she always left the parlour first
- Claim: Carmelites had a half-hour sand-glass for parlour visits. When the last grain fell, Thérèse greeted the visitors graciously, closed grille and curtain, and left at once. With her sisters she was always the first to go. When the others were there she gladly stayed silent. She was careful to accept or ask nothing against strict poverty.

- Claim: At the retreat at the start of her novitiate, Pichon was struck that, contrary to appearances, God did not give her the sweetness of affective piety. He led her by dryness, privation and inner trials. These never showed in a sad or worried face; she stayed serene and even-tempered.

### F-EW-556 — Auriault: she "obeyed right or wrong"
- Claim: Auriault says her surrender to God appeared in unreserved obedience to all her superiors' directions; "one could say she obeyed right or wrong", because she always saw God's fatherly will behind creatures.
- When / where: 1888–1897
- People: Thérèse
- Witness: Auriault, from reading
- Source: PA-04, p. 288
- New to English readers: no
- Confidence: medium — interpretation

### F-EW-557 — Gonzague said she tested Thérèse with feigned indifference and severity
- Claim: Mère Marie de Gonzague told Godefroy that, to train Thérèse's virtue, she deliberately showed her a kind of indifference and some appearance of severity. She said this apparent rebuff was very painful to Thérèse in the first years; later Thérèse mastered it and made it a joyful sacrifice, and never failed in obedience.
- When / where: 1888–c. 1893, Lisieux Carmel
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague; Thérèse
- Witness: Godefroy heard it from Gonzague herself
- Source: PO-24, f. 1218v, session 78, 24 May 1911; PA-26, f. 25v, 33r ("the prioress who loved her much treated her with a severity that seemed extraordinary", "to form her to a virile virtue")
- Original words: « elle s'étudiait à l'éprouver, en affectant à son égard une sorte d'indifférence et quelque apparence de sévérité »
- Translation: she made a point of testing her, putting on toward her a kind of indifference and some show of severity
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high — Gonzague's own account, reported first-hand
- Notes: Valuable: the prioress's own explanation of her coldness.
- Extraction ID: H-F87
### F-EW-558 — "A little saint" spoken of in Lisieux during her life
- Claim: During her life Godefroy often heard her praised as a marvel of virtue, at the Saint-Jacques presbytery, at the Carmel and elsewhere. People said there was "a little saint" at the Carmel, but no one foresaw her fame.
- When / where: 1888–1897, Lisieux
- People: Godefroy; the Saint-Jacques clergy
- Witness: Godefroy, heard
- Source: PO-24, f. 1220r
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium — memory after the fact
- Notes: In PA-26 (f. 24r) he says that while she lived he had the highest opinion of her "but without sensing the marvels of holiness revealed later"; she hid her virtues.
- Extraction ID: H-F97
### F-EW-559 — She died of a weak constitution, not of penance
- Claim: Asked about excess, Godefroy said he never heard of her exceeding in bodily mortification; she was guided in all by obedience. If she died young, it was from weakness of constitution, not excessive mortification. He believed she "carried the germ of the illness for several years".
- When / where: 1888–1897
- People: Thérèse
- Witness: Godefroy's opinion
- Source: PA-26, f. 34r (48th question) and 35r
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium
- Notes: He also says she refused dispensations in her youth and begged to follow the whole Rule (f. 32v).
- Extraction ID: H-F106
### F-EW-560 — She loved used and worn things
- Claim: Godefroy says she was known at Lisieux for fidelity to poverty to the smallest detail; she preferred things others had used, and the simplest and most worn objects.

- People: Thérèse
- Witness: Godefroy
- Source: PA-26, f. 33r (44th question)

### F-EW-561 — Opposition: Gonzague praised the novice in front of her
- Claim: Opposition: Once during her novitiate Domin saw Thérèse in the parlour with Mère Marie de Gonzague, who praised the little novice's generosity in her presence. Domin was surprised and thought it imprudent to praise a young person to her face.
- When / where: 1888–1890, Carmel parlour
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague; Thérèse; Domin
- Witness: Domin SAW and heard
- Source: PO-26, f. 1236v
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high — first-hand
- Notes: Contrasts with Gonzague's claim that she was deliberately cold and severe to her (F-EW-557). Domin later concluded her humility was firm since the praise did not shake her.

### F-EW-562 — Opposition: Domin called it "bluff" and stayed away from the Carmel
- Claim: Opposition: Domin thought Youf's praise was "bluff", as the English say. As a result he hardly went to see her at the Carmel; he kept a great reserve because he found the opinion of "this child" exaggerated and did not want to join the chorus of praise. He now says he was wrong.
- When / where: 1888–1897
- People: Domin
- Witness: Domin about himself
- Source: PO-26, f. 1237r; editors' intro (he "no longer set foot in the Carmel")
- Original words: « je trouvais exagérée l'opinion que l'on avait de cette enfant »
- Translation: I found the opinion people had of this child exaggerated
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high — first-hand, against interest
- Notes: The editors call his testimony "made of sincerity". Reasons: fear of flattery; distrust of Martin, Gonzague and Youf's praise.
- Extraction ID: H-F131
### F-EW-563 — Sr St Vincent de Paul: early friction
- Claim: From Thérèse's entry there was friction with Sr St Vincent de Paul, a lay sister 32 years older. Thérèse wrote to Sr Agnès that she had had pain "at Sr St Vincent de Paul's" that morning and went away with a heavy heart (letter LT 76).
- When / where: 1888–1889 (LT 76); Lisieux Carmel
- People: Sr St Vincent de Paul (lay sister, cook); Thérèse; Sr Agnès de Jésus
- Witness: Thérèse's own letter, cited by editors

- Claim: Sr Marie-Philomène wrote that sometimes she would have liked to see Thérèse show "a little more eagerness" for her work assignments, though she concluded that she herself was wrong and that Thérèse, calmly, did more and better.

- Claim: In the novitiate Thérèse had a companion, a young lay sister of very difficult character, who was jealous of her virtue and always threw barbs at her. Thérèse sat next to her most often and won her over, gaining great influence on her.

### F-EW-568 — Gonzague cold to the postulant
- Claim: Marie du Sacré-Cœur wrote that Gonzague was used to being caressed and flattered by everyone. Because Thérèse never sought herself, she passed unnoticed or was met with coldness. Gonzague showed no sympathy for the young postulant, often scolding her or ignoring her.
- When / where: 1888
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague; Thérèse; Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur

### F-EW-569 — Direction sessions: scolded almost the whole hour
- Claim: In her direction sessions with Gonzague (about an hour), Thérèse was scolded almost the whole time. What hurt her most was not understanding how to correct her faults. She left with a torn heart.
- When / where: 1888–1890
- People: Gonzague; Thérèse
- Witness: Sr Marie des Anges (novice mistress) and Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, both state it

### F-EW-570 — "What kind of novice has to be sent for a walk every day?"
- Claim: Marie des Anges sent the postulant to weed in the garden every day at 4.30 to get fresh air. Thérèse disliked it but said nothing. Gonzague often met her there and said: what kind of novice must be sent every day for a walk? She was also heard to say: "this child does absolutely nothing!" Thérèse smiled and did not explain she was going to work.

- People: Gonzague; Thérèse; Sr Marie des Anges; Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur

- Claim: Several nuns say Thérèse never sought the company of her three sisters at recreation, without affecting to flee them. She went with any sister, most often with one who was alone or neglected.

- People: Thérèse; Martin sisters

### F-EW-575 — Mère Geneviève scolded her for not greeting Mère Agnès
- Claim: After a retreat Thérèse came to recreation without having said a single good-day to Mère Agnès, who was hurt. Mère Agnès expected her at least to sit near her, but Thérèse sat by the first sister she met. This was told to Mère Geneviève, who scolded her: that was not true charity.

- Claim: In their free-talk days (licences), Thérèse often told Marie-Philomène it hurt her that she was too afraid of God. ### F-EW-579 — She ate the leftovers nobody wanted
- Claim: When the kitchen had leftovers they did not know whom to give to, they sent them to Thérèse, who ate them without complaint. ### F-EW-581 — The pretty little jug exchanged for a chipped one
- Claim: As a postulant she liked neat things and to have everything to hand for work. Gradually she gave up her pretty little cell jug for a big chipped one, and came to love the ugliest, least handy things. One evening her lamp was taken by mistake just when she had much to do; she was tempted to complain but kept silence.

### F-EW-583 — Painting and poetry only with permission
- Claim: Thérèse would not ask other sisters to teach her painting without Marie des Anges's permission; nor write poems. She brought her first poem, on "the virginal milk of Mary", with great joy. - Claim: As a postulant she spared no effort to build a crib in the novitiate, carrying heavy stones from quite far. Another crib, which had failed the previous year and was meant as a surprise for her, was chosen instead, so hers would not be seen by the community. She showed no disappointment.

- Claim: In the small Christmas and Easter plays composed by Sr Agnès, the postulant and novice recited so movingly that it brought tears. One Christmas, playing the Virgin, she held the Child Jesus so recollected that "if He had been alive" she could not have been more so.

### F-EW-587 — Leaving the needle mid-stitch at the bell
- Claim: At the first sound of the bell she left everything, even an interesting conversation; if sewing she did not finish the stitch, leaving the needle where the bell found it. She was usually first in choir, and said she had the blessing of the community's angel.

### F-EW-588 — Sweeping the chapel; "Are you there, Jesus?"
- Claim: While Thérèse and Marthe were still novices, and no one else was available, they swept the chapel for some weeks. One day, in an impulse of love, Thérèse knelt on the altar, knocked at the tabernacle door and said: "Are you there, Jesus? Answer me, I beg you!" She rested her head on the little gilded door for some moments, then got up with her face as if transfigured.

- People: Thérèse; Sr Marthe

- Claim: The witness wrote that as soon as they met they felt "an irresistible attraction" for each other, bound by devotion to the Holy Childhood, especially the Child at his Mother's breast. They spoke little: "is it possible to love each other so much and speak so little?"

### F-EW-592 — Louis's gift of the cathedral high altar; family criticism
- Claim: In 1888 Canon Rohée announced from the pulpit of Saint-Pierre, Lisieux, that 10,000 francs would buy a new altar. M. Martin brought the sum at once and required secrecy; no parishioner knew the donor, but M. Guérin learned it. Some family members criticised Louis: too big a gift for his means, harming his children. Thérèse rejoiced: after giving all his daughters to God, it was natural he should give an altar for their immolation and his own.

- Claim: As a young novice decorating the statue, she never used scented flowers, not even a violet, because perfume bothered one of the old mothers. ### F-EW-596 — "If I fall, they'll see it!"
- Claim: As postulant and novice she did not say when she was ill unless ordered. - Claim: One evening in Thérèse's postulancy, toward the end of the Great Silence, Marie des Anges went to her cell to console her in a great sorrow. ### F-EW-2593 — Isidore Guérin inherits La Musse and sells the pharmacy (Aug–Dec 1888)
- Claim: Auguste David, retired notary of Évreux, a widower without children and rich, chose his second cousins the Fournet children as heirs. Near death at 75 he called Isidore Guérin to his 'sumptuous house of La Musse'; Isidore brought a crucifix, the one thing missing from the decoration, and reconciled the old man with God. David died on 22 Aug 1888 saying: 'Guérin, I owe you my salvation.' As universal legatee Isidore untangled the will: a princely town house at Évreux, a summer residence two leagues away, art furniture, securities and lands, shared amicably with the Maudelonde family with no quarrel. La Musse (a former seigneurial domain of 41 hectares of gardens, woods and parks above the valley of the Iton) was left undivided for both families to use by turns in summer. On 8 Dec 1888 Isidore sold the pharmacy; on 20 Apr 1889 he bought a mansion at 19 rue Paul Banaston in Lisieux, occupying it towards the end of 1889.
- When / where: 22 Aug 1888 – end 1889, Évreux, La Musse, Lisieux
- People: Auguste David; Isidore Guérin; the Maudelonde and Guérin families
- Witness: Fr Piat (1967), who questioned Thérèse's sisters at the parlour; modern author, not an eyewitness
- Source: Extract from Fr Stéphane-Joseph Piat OFM, biography of Marie Guérin (Office Central de Lisieux, 1967), on the Carmel site, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/au-carmel-du-temps-de-therese/la-communaute/soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/biographie-de-soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/ — section 'La victoire sur le monde'; rights: facts-only
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high
- Notes: This wealth paid for the family's later gifts to the Carmel (see the next card) and put the Guérins in a 'notable' class. Louis Martin died at La Musse on 29 Jul 1894.
- Extraction ID: N-F144

What an admirable sight that of this new Abraham!... We are pygmies beside this man.' ### F-EW-2601 — Marie Guérin at La Musse: the squirrel letter and Jeanne's wedding (1 Oct 1890)
- Claim: Piat says Marie did not enjoy the family's new wealth; she fled the society visits when propriety allowed, was a 'sunbeam' at home but reserved with strangers. She was happiest in the solitary walks of La Musse or perched on an oak. He quotes her letter to Léonie from a tree branch: no blotting paper needed, the sun does that; a squirrel mocks her; the birds ask where Léonie is; the river Iton flows taciturn because it cannot reflect Léonie's face; the horse Bichette raises its head to the loft window looking for 'her amazon'; the twenty hares of the park have almost all vanished. At her sister Jeanne's wedding on 1 Oct 1890, obliged to play a show role, she put on the shimmering dress with complete indifference and without a look at the mirror. This wedding, Piat notes, was the occasion for Thérèse to write the announcement of her union with the divine Spouse.
- When / where: 1888–1 Oct 1890, La Musse and Lisieux
- People: Marie Guérin; Léonie Martin; Jeanne Guérin; Francis La Néele; Thérèse

### F-EW-2603 — Marie Guérin's spiritual life before Carmel: woods at La Musse, the poor, and Gonzague's favour
- Claim: Each year, in a quiet period, Marie made a retreat at La Musse, going into the woods four times a day for half an hour of prayer; she never missed her monthly retreat, insisting on preparation for death. With Céline and friends she attended weekly sewing meetings to make clothes for the poorest families. Every fortnight she went to the Carmel for half an hour at the parlour. Gonzague had taken her into affection and, Piat says, was open-minded in admitting Thérèse at 15, and equally favourable to Céline and then Marie, an unusual gathering of five members of one family in a community limited by rule to 21 and, by special leave, then 27. Marie also poured out to Agnès and to Thérèse, whom she still called 'the little Queen'. She often wrote to Carmel in secret, because her parents did not favour a tendency to mysticism.
- When / where: 1888–1895, La Musse and Lisieux
- People: Marie Guérin; Céline Martin; Gonzague; Agnès; Thérèse
- Witness: Fr Piat (1967), who questioned Thérèse's sisters at the parlour; modern author, not an eyewitness
- Source: Extract from Fr Stéphane-Joseph Piat OFM, biography of Marie Guérin (Office Central de Lisieux, 1967), on the Carmel site, https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/au-carmel-du-temps-de-therese/la-communaute/soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/biographie-de-soeur-marie-de-leucharistie/ — section 'La victoire sur le monde'; rights: facts-only

### F-LF-162 — Louis's first stroke, 1 May 1887
- Claim: Céline wrote in 1909 that Louis's first "attack of paralysis" came on 1 May 1887. It was very violent and struck the whole left side, and he recovered fairly quickly. A few weeks later he had two more, similar but milder. Laveille says he looked healthy but friends feared a return, and that a poisonous fly sting while fishing left a small growth on his neck that no remedy removed and sometimes gave great pain. In June 1887 Louis, Léonie, Céline and Thérèse visited the International Maritime Exhibition at Le Havre.

### F-LF-164 — Pranzini: newspapers forbidden, first prayer for a sinner
- Claim: Louis did not let his daughters read newspapers, not even *La Croix*, to which he subscribed, but news was discussed at home. Laveille places "the latter part of June 1887" as the time when all spoke of the murder of two women and a girl in the rue Montaigu, Paris, by Henri Pranzini, who showed no repentance and refused religious help. Thérèse resolved to save his soul and offered "the infinite merits of Our Saviour". Her prayer: "My God, I feel assured that You will pardon the unfortunate Pranzini… But this is my first sinner; for that reason I ask but a sign of repentance for my own consolation" (HA ch. v p.76). She then allowed herself to glance at the newspaper daily, against her father's rule.
- When / where: summer 1887; Lisieux
- People: Thérèse; Louis; Henri Pranzini
- Source: LAV p.154–155 (HA ch. v p.76)
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial (baseline: Pranzini kissed the crucifix)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Laveille dates the prayer to late June 1887 (his own reckoning).
### F-LF-165 — Pranzini's execution as *La Croix* reported it, 1 Sep 1887
- Claim: *La Croix* of 1 Sep 1887 reported the execution. Pranzini was handed to the executioner Deibler and his assistants, his hair cut and neck bared. It was "two minutes to five, while the birds sang in the trees around". He appeared with a "livid face", pushed away the priest and the executioners. The chaplain stood in front to hide the machine. Deibler pushed him onto the guillotine and an assistant held his head by the hair. "But before the final stroke… he asked for the chaplain's crucifix, and three times he kissed it." The report ends that if human justice is satisfied, perhaps this last kiss also satisfied Divine Justice "which demands, above all, repentance". Thérèse read it, was in tears and had to run away. She wrote that she had "the desired sign" and that the lips of "mon premier enfant" were pressed to the Divine Wounds (HA ch. v p.76–77).
- When / where: 31 Aug / 1 Sep 1887; Paris; Lisieux
- People: Pranzini; Deibler; the chaplain; Thérèse
- Source: LAV p.155–156 (La Croix, 1 Sep 1887)
- Source type: official (press report) / own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high for the wording as Laveille prints it
- Notes: The date of the execution itself is not stated in the pages read; *La Croix* is dated 1 Sep 1887. Surprise: the report also says an assistant lifted the severed head "by one ear". Thérèse read it against her father's rule.
### F-LF-166 — Why Carmel: to suffer and to pray for priests
- Claim: Thérèse weighed being a missionary nun, a nursing sister, or a helper of priests in parish work, and an inner voice pointed to the contemplative life. Céline testified that Thérèse chose Carmel in order to suffer more and win more souls: it is harder for nature to work without seeing the fruit, encouragement or distraction. Thérèse wished "to become a prisoner as soon as possible, in order to give the beauty and freedom of heaven to souls", and to pray for priests. On the Rome pilgrimage a pilgrim lent her annals of missionary nuns; she gave them to Céline unread, saying she wished to be hidden in a cloister. She took a maxim of St John of the Cross without any director: the smallest movement of pure love is more useful to the Church than all works together.
- When / where: 1887; Lisieux
- People: Thérèse; Céline; John of the Cross
- Source: LAV p.156–157 (Céline, Process of the Ordinary p.204)
- Source type: eyewitness / own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high for the testimony as printed
- Notes: Connection to other saints: John of the Cross.
### F-LF-167 — Her reading and her allies, 1887
- Claim: The *Imitation of Christ* was her constant companion; she learned it by heart in a few months and could repeat whole chapters. Abbé Arminjon's *Conferences on the End of the World and the Mysteries of the Future Life* brought "a happiness not of earth" (HA ch. v p.78). Only Pauline encouraged her vocation (HA p.81). Marie, as eldest sister, thought her too young. Speaking through the grille was rare and hard. Céline, who wished for religious life, "suppressing her own desire", agreed to let Thérèse go first. Evenings the two sat hand in hand in the Belvedere and talked of Carmel, quoting the Song of Songs 8:1. Louis had both girls learn housekeeping. Thérèse went to Mass each morning and, unusually for the time, her confessor let her communicate several times a week, in the 15th-century apse chapel of Saint-Pierre. Laveille adds the tradition, "sometimes denied", that Bishop Cauchon built it in atonement for his part in Joan of Arc's trial.
- When / where: 1887; Lisieux
- People: Thérèse; Pauline; Marie; Céline; Abbé Arminjon

- Rights: public-domain

### F-LF-168 — Pentecost 1887: telling her father, the white flower
- Claim: Louis was then 63–64 (Laveille says sixty-four). Thérèse, fourteen and a half, decided to enter Carmel at Christmas, the anniversary of her "conversion", and chose Pentecost (29 May 1887) to tell him. After the cathedral ceremonies and Vespers, Louis sat "a little fatigued" at the garden entrance at the back of the house. She sat beside him with tears and no words. He drew her to his heart: "What is it, my little Queen? Tell me…" He rose to hide his emotion and walked slowly with his arm round her. She told him. He wept, said she was very young for so grave a decision, and listened to her calm reasons. He went to a wall where tiny white lily-like flowers grew, plucked one and gave it to her as a sign of purity. A white marble group of the scene stands now in the garden of Les Buissonnets.
- When / where: Pentecost, 29 May 1887; garden of Les Buissonnets
- People: Louis; Thérèse
- Source: LAV p.161–162 (HA ch. v); date 29 May from the handoff (calendar)
- Source type: own-writing (as reported)
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high for the scene; the sunset and bird details in Laveille are his embellishment (not recorded here).
### F-LF-169 — Uncle Guérin says no, then yes
- Claim: Since Zélie's death Isidore Guérin was Thérèse's guardian and his consent was needed. He was surprised and at first opposed: "This would be… a unique case in the whole of France. It would, in fact, be almost a scandal for a child of fifteen years to enter Carmel." He would oppose it and said "a miracle would be necessary" to change his mind. For three days Thérèse felt abandoned by God and man. On the fourth day he said, "A miracle is no longer necessary… I have prayed to God to give me an unbiassed inclination of heart… You are a little privileged flower that the Saviour wills to cull for Himself; I will not stand in opposition."
- When / where: Jun 1887; Lisieux
- People: Isidore Guérin; Thérèse
- Source: LAV p.162–163
- Source type: own-writing (as reported)
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
