- Notes: Opponent type: family guardian; reason: her age ("a scandal").
### F-LF-170 — Canon Delatroëtte's refusals
- Claim: The Prioress, Mère Marie de Gonzague, was now willing but had to consult the community's ecclesiastical superior, Canon Delatroëtte, parish priest of Saint-Jacques, "of lively faith… but with rigid, definitely moulded convictions". He held that no one should enter Carmel before twenty-one. Laveille notes the Carmelite Constitutions set no age limit for postulants; they required only that profession come after the seventeenth year. When the Canon visited the infirmary of the foundress, Mother Geneviève, the petition was renewed. He exploded: "Again this young girl… To hear you talk, one would think that the salvation of the community depended on the entrance of this child… Let her remain with her father until she has reached her majority." (Mère Agnès, Apostolic Process p.161.) Louis then took Thérèse to the presbytery, where a "sharp and decisive No" cut short her speech. At parting the Canon said he was only the Bishop's delegate: "If he allows you to enter, I shall have no more to say." They left in torrents of rain, and Louis at once offered to take her to Bayeux.
- When / where: Sep–Oct 1887; Lisieux Carmel; Saint-Jacques presbytery
- People: Canon Delatroëtte; Mère Marie de Gonzague; Mother Geneviève; Louis; Thérèse
- Source: LAV p.163–165
- Source type: eyewitness (Mère Agnès) / early-life
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high for the words as printed
- Notes: Opponent type: ecclesiastical superior; reason: age (twenty-one). His name was checked on the page image (OCR "Delatréette").
### F-LF-171 — Bayeux, 31 October 1887: "diamonds" and the armchair
- Claim: Bishop Hugonin of Bayeux was "gentle and benevolent", studious and old, and left much administration to his vicars general. Abbé Révérony, vicar general, had refused the episcopate, and the Bishop rarely acted without him. Révérony fixed 31 Oct 1887 for Louis's interview. It was the first time Thérèse visited anyone without her sisters. To look older she put up her hair, which until then hung in natural curls. Thérèse wrote that Révérony said, seeing her tears, "Ah, I see diamonds. You must not show these to Monseigneur." Through large state rooms she felt "as insignificant as a tiny ant". Three enormous armchairs stood before a bright fire. Révérony told her to take the middle one "to show that I was capable of obedience", and she sat "buried in a monumental seat where four girls like me might sit at ease". Asked if she had long wished for Carmel, she said, "Oh yes, Monseigneur… a very long time." Révérony smiled: "certainly not as long as fifteen years." She said, "from the age of three I have desired to give myself to the good God." Louis said they were going to Rome and she would speak to the Pope. She "did more than show my diamonds… I gave them", shedding tears. The Bishop caressed her, a favour "no other child had ever received from him", and said, "All is not lost, little one… You shall certainly receive my reply in Italy." She had achieved nothing.
- When / where: 31 Oct 1887; the Bishop's house, Bayeux
- People: Mgr Hugonin; Abbé Révérony; Louis; Thérèse
- Source: LAV p.165–168 (HA ch. v pp.89–91; Révérony: anonymous *Vie de l'Abbé J.-M. Révérony*, Montligeon 1900)
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high for her account
- Notes: Laveille infers the Bishop and Vicar General thought the step "inopportune and unusual"; that is his inference. Opponent types: Bishop (cautious), vicar general (guarded, later helped).
### F-LF-172 — The pilgrimage: who organised it and how it was paid for
- Claim: The diocesan pilgrimage honoured Leo XIII's priestly jubilee. Laveille says Mgr Germain, Bishop of Coutances and a strong protester against the Italian government, "apparently" started it, and Abbé Legoux, vicar general of Coutances and organiser of pilgrimages, recruited clergy and wealthy families. Bayeux sent Révérony at the head of its people. The "Agence Lubin" provided, "for an agreed sum payable in advance", railway, hotels, meals and sight-seeing by vehicle. Laveille calls it both an act of loyalty and "a magnificent pleasure trip". Carmel dates: 4 Nov – 2 Dec 1887. Numbers: 197 pilgrims left Paris for Lucerne (a Caen magistrate); "all 200" (Abbé Hamel); 194 signatures on the thank-you address to Mgr Germain (23 Nov 1887); "70 or 80 priests" (Abbé Huet). Context: after 1870 French pilgrimages to the "prisoner of the Vatican" mixed prayer for the Pope with national penance (Brennan 2000; facts only).

- People: Mgr Germain; Abbé Legoux; Abbé Révérony; Abbé Louis Huet; Louis; Céline; Thérèse

### F-LF-173 — Paris to Milan: nobles, cards, and "pharisees"
- Claim: On 4 Nov 1887 Louis left Lisieux with Céline and Thérèse for Paris (Carmel: 3 a.m.). The carriages carried religious emblems and pilgrims displayed noble titles. Thérèse wrote that these titles seemed "smoke and vanity", citing the *Imitation*. She also wrote that in a month among priests she saw they were "still subject to human weakness", so prayer for priests came first (HA ch. vi p.95). Each compartment had a saint as patron; Mgr Legoux, seeing the Lisieux three, named theirs St Martin. In the plains of Champagne cards took more hours than prayer, even in Louis's compartment. Asked to play, Louis declined; a vexed passenger cried, "Fortunately, pharisees are rare." Louis said nothing and soon shook the man's hand. In Paris on 6–7 Nov the pilgrims were consecrated to the Sacred Heart at Montmartre. Thérèse knelt before Our Lady of Victories (see F-LF-143). The party went by Basel (a few hours at the station) to Lucerne, where the scenery drew a cry of admiration. Thérèse wrote she would recall this "little corner of the heavens" in Carmel (HA p.97). In Milan they heard Mass at St Charles Borromeo's tomb; the sisters climbed the dome; Thérèse called the Campo Santo "masterpieces" and rebuked an old grumbler: "he had better have stayed home".
- When / where: 4–9 Nov 1887; Paris, Basel, Lucerne, Milan
- People: Louis; Céline; Thérèse; Mgr Legoux; Mgr Germain
- Source: LAV p.169–175 (HA ch. vi pp.95–98); Carmel timetable (URL in F-LF-172)

- Notes: Connection to other saints: Martin of Tours (compartment patron); Charles Borromeo.
### F-LF-174 — Venice, Padua, Bologna, Loreto
- Claim: They reached Venice at about ten at night and were taken by gondola in the dark to the pre-booked hotel. Thérèse said the city "has many charms, but it is melancholy". She was struck with horror and pity at the underground prisons of the Doges' palace. Laveille states in a footnote that she stayed at the Hotel della Luna, "where the author of this book, a member of the same pilgrimage, also stayed. Without knowing her, he probably passed her often in the corridors or on the stairs." At Padua they saw the relic of St Anthony. At Bologna they hurried to the church of St Catherine, where the body shows the imprint of the Infant's kiss; Thérèse later told a sister that the embalmed body was "not very attractive". At Loreto she touched the "bowl of the Infant Jesus" with her rosary, and she and Céline, "by a holy daring", received Communion inside the Santa Casa (Sunday 13 Nov by the Carmel timetable).
- When / where: 9–13 Nov 1887; Venice, Padua, Bologna, Loreto
- People: Louis; Céline; Thérèse; Laveille (fellow pilgrim)

### F-LF-175 — Arrival in Rome; Révérony's "inquisitorial eye"
- Claim: They reached Rome at night, and Thérèse was asleep in a corner when porters cried "Roma, Roma!" The Martins lodged at the Hôtel du Sud, Via Capo le Case, where a plaque was placed on 17 May 1925. Mgr Germain, Mgr Legoux and most Coutances pilgrims stayed at the Hôtel Minerva. Révérony watched Thérèse's actions with what Laveille calls "an inquisitorial eye" at every halt since Normandy, and she was embarrassed. Pilgrims were split into five groups; the Martins' group began with the Campagna.

### F-LF-176 — The Coliseum: over the barrier for martyrs' dust
- Claim: Thérèse and Céline wanted to kiss the arena ground and take dust stained with martyrs' blood. The original soil lay at least 25 feet down; one dug-out part was a gaping hole behind a strong barrier. Thérèse said, "Come, follow me… we shall be able to get down." They climbed over crumbling ruins while Louis, astonished, called from a distance, "but we no longer heard anything". Céline, who had listened to the guide, found the stone marked with a cross where the martyrs fought. They knelt. Thérèse wrote she asked "that I, too, might be a martyr for Jesus" and felt her prayer heard. They took small stones. Louis, seeing them delighted, "could not chide us"; she saw he was proud of their courage (HA ch. vi p.102).
- When / where: mid-Nov 1887; the Coliseum, Rome
- People: Thérèse; Céline; Louis

### F-LF-177 — Catacombs of St Calixtus: St Cecilia, and a fragment for Pauline
- Claim: A Norman Trappist, Père Marie-Bernard, guided them by torchlight in the catacombs of St Calixtus. Thérèse and Céline each lay in turn in the stone loculus where St Cecilia's body was found. Thérèse wrote that Cecilia "became my saint of predilection, my intimate confidante", for her "perfect abandonment to God" and "unlimited confidence" (HA ch. vi p.103). At the tomb of St Agnes she took a small piece of ancient mosaic as a souvenir for Pauline, Sr Agnès de Jésus.

### F-LF-178 — Six days of sightseeing and the nail at Santa Croce
- Claim: The pilgrims planned to see Rome's wonders in six days, hurried by guides with little time for prayer, in constant rain and a "leaden sky". Thérèse does not mention St Peter's, the Scala Santa or the Mamertine prison in her autobiography; she does mention Holy Cross in Jerusalem (Santa Croce). There, as the religious in charge of the relics was about to replace them on the altar, she asked if she might touch them. He said yes but doubted she could. She put her little finger through an opening in the reliquary and touched one of the nails. She wrote she acted "like a child who believes that everything is allowed her" (HA ch. vi p.110).
- When / where: 13–21 Nov 1887; Rome
- People: Thérèse; an unnamed religious

### F-LF-179 — Audience with Leo XIII, Sunday 20 November 1887
- Claim: Early on 20 Nov, agency carriages fetched groups in a steady downpour. At 7.30 a.m. pilgrims assembled under Mgr Germain and Abbé Révérony, with the bishops of Nantes, Séez and Vannes, in a hall hung with red damask. At 8 a.m. Leo XIII entered in a red mantle over a white woollen cassock, deathly pale, with trembling hands, and said Mass slowly with an Italian accent. In the audience chamber he sat on an ordinary armchair on a low step, took each pilgrim's hand, said a few words and gave a medal. Laveille footnotes that Mgr Germain presented him as author of a manual of Thomistic philosophy and the Pope praised him. Bayeux pilgrims came next, presented by Révérony; over a hundred people were present. As the two sisters approached "with calm and resolute faces beneath their black mantillas", Révérony "announced in a loud voice that he absolutely forbade anyone to speak to the Holy Father". Thérèse looked at Céline, who said, "Speak!" Thérèse kissed the Pope's foot, took his hand, in tears: "Most Holy Father, I have a great favour to ask… in honour of your Jubilee, permit me to enter Carmel at fifteen." The Pope bent his head to her mantilla. Révérony said, "this is a child who desires the life of Carmel, but the superiors are, at the moment, considering the question." Leo XIII: "Well, my child, do whatever the superiors shall decide." Thérèse, hands on his knees: "O Holy Father, if you only said yes everyone else would agree." He looked at her fixedly and said gravely: "Well, my child, you shall enter if it be God's will." She tried to ask again; two noble guards told her to rise and, when she stayed, took her by the arms, and Révérony had to help before she left the Pope's knees. Louis had received his blessing earlier and met her at the door "bathed in tears, which she made no effort to dry".
- When / where: 20 Nov 1887; the Vatican
- People: Leo XIII; Thérèse; Céline; Louis; Révérony; Mgr Germain; two noble guards
- Source: LAV p.182–185 (audience dialogue has no footnote in Laveille; Laveille was present in the same pilgrimage and audience)
- Source type: early-life (Laveille, eyewitness of the hall; dialogue from Thérèse and family)
- Rights: public-domain
- Other sources: HA ch. vi (Thérèse's own account; own-writings part)
- New to English readers: partial (baseline: she asked the Pope)
- Confidence: high for the main exchange; medium for exact wording (Laveille's version is not footnoted).
- Notes: Laveille's account of the Pope's appearance may come from his own memory. Opponent: Révérony, at the moment of the audience. Surprise: the guards lifted her from the Pope's knees. Carmel notes that Thérèse's comment in LT 36 that the Pope seemed dead was later scraped off the letter (web-context [T8]).
### F-LF-180 — After the audience: Brother Siméon, "That never happens in Italy"
- Claim: A few days later (Carmel: 22 Nov 1887, while the daughters were at Naples/Pompeii) Louis visited Brother Siméon, founder and superior of St Joseph's college, Rome. He met Révérony there and "gently reproached" him for not helping Thérèse, and told Brother Siméon of her attempt. Brother Siméon said, "That never happens in Italy." Révérony did not defend himself; Laveille says he believed he followed his bishop's wishes. Thérèse had earlier offered herself to the Infant Jesus as his "little ball" to be tossed or pierced as he pleased.
- When / where: 22 Nov 1887; Rome
- People: Louis; Brother Siméon; Révérony
- Source: LAV p.185–186; Carmel Céline account https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/la-vie-de-sainte-therese-de-lisieux/voyage-a-rome/recits-de-celine/ (date, via helper)

### F-LF-181 — Naples and Pompeii, 21–22 November 1887
- Claim: The day after the audience the pilgrims left early and passed Aquino and Monte Cassino. They saw Pompeii in a rain shower with a "merciless" guide, and Thérèse wished to wander alone among the ruins. At Naples she saw the Bay and Vesuvius and the monastery of San Martino. She wrote that joy is not found in what surrounds us but in the soul: "in the depths of an obscure prison as well as in a royal palace" (HA ch. vi p.109). Coming down the hill from San Martino the horses bolted, and she thought her guardian angel brought them safely to the hotel.

### F-LF-182 — Home by Assisi and Florence
- Claim: The party left Rome on 24 Nov at 6 a.m. At an early stop Mgr Legoux came to their carriage and asked, "Well, how is our little Carmelite?" She saw that all the pilgrims knew her secret. Laveille says that since Rome he had heard "this young girl of Lisieux" discussed in his own compartment with curiosity and admiration, and he never met her, though "he had lived for a whole month in her immediate vicinity". At Assisi she mislaid some articles of dress at the convent and missed the carriage to the station. Only Révérony's carriage was left, full. He had a companion sit with the driver and put her opposite him in the best seat. She felt "like a squirrel caught in a trap". He spoke of Carmel and promised to do all he could to help her enter at fifteen. She wrote that she then lost confidence in creatures and relied on God alone. At Florence, while others visited the Uffizi, she went to the convent of St Mary Magdalen de' Pazzi and asked at her tomb to enter Carmel. Her hand alone was small enough to pass through the grating around the body, so she touched pilgrims' rosaries to the relics. The pilgrims then passed Pisa and Genoa, along the sea, and prayed at Notre-Dame de la Garde (Marseille) and Fourvière (Lyon). They reached Paris on 2 Dec 1887.
- When / where: 24 Nov – 2 Dec 1887; Assisi, Florence, Marseille, Lyon, Paris
- People: Thérèse; Céline; Mgr Legoux; Révérony; Laveille; Mary Magdalen de' Pazzi
- Source: LAV p.187–190; Carmel timetable (URL in F-LF-172) for Nice–Marseille–Lyon–Paris dates
- Source type: own-writing / early-life / eyewitness (Laveille)
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The Carmel timetable has arrival in Paris at 1 a.m. on 2 Dec and Lisieux at 16.30 that day. Connection to other saints: Mary Magdalen de' Pazzi.
### F-LF-183 — Waiting: the Bishop's letter and the delay until after Lent
- Claim: Louis offered to take her to Jerusalem, "probably during the journey from Paris to Lisieux"; she refused, wanting a cell. She still hoped to enter on 25 Dec. Pauline reminded her that the Bishop had promised a written reply and advised her to write. She wrote and Mgr Hugonin stayed silent. On 1 Jan 1888 a letter came from the Prioress: the Bishop had written to the monastery on 28 Dec 1887 allowing her immediate entry, but Mère Marie de Gonzague thought it better to wait until after Lent. Thérèse later learned this was to soothe Delatroëtte, who still opposed, and to spare her a Carmelite Lent at the start. It was "at first extremely painful"; she used the months to practise self-forgetting.
- When / where: 2 Dec 1887 – Lent 1888; Lisieux
- People: Louis; Pauline; Mère Marie de Gonzague; Mgr Hugonin; Delatroëtte; Thérèse

- Source type: early-life
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes

- Notes: Opponent reason: the prioress's deference to Delatroëtte. Laveille's offer of Jerusalem: the motive is his guess.

## Part 8 — The Carmel, and Louis's illness (1888–1894)
### F-LF-186 — Last days at Les Buissonnets: the lamb and the last dinner
- Claim: Entry was fixed for 9 April 1888. Léonie had come home after leaving the Poor Clares because of poor health. Louis, on a country walk, brought Thérèse a one-day-old white lamb, and the sisters were delighted; it died the same day. Thérèse wrote to Marie that we should not become attached to anything on earth, even things most innocent, "for they fail us at the moment we least think". On the evening of 8 April the family had a last dinner at the oak table: Louis, Léonie, Céline, Thérèse, Isidore and Céline Guérin, Jeanne and Marie Guérin. Thérèse wrote that "words of the deepest tenderness are on all lips, as though to make the sacrifice of separation more keenly felt" (HA ch. vii p.116).
- When / where: 8–9 Apr 1888; Les Buissonnets
- People: Louis; Léonie; Céline; Thérèse; the Guérins
- Source: LAV p.194–195
- Source type: own-writing (as reported) / early-life
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: The lamb is in Laveille only.
### F-LF-187 — Entry, 9 April 1888
- Claim: Thérèse wrote that after a last look at Les Buissonnets she went to Mass with her family. At Communion "I heard nothing but the sound of weeping." She shed no tears. As she walked ahead to the cloister door her heart beat so hard she wondered if she would die. She knelt before Louis for his blessing; "he too knelt and, weeping, he blessed me" (HA ch. vii p.116). Léonie testified that Thérèse alone was calm, and that she told her to think well because religious life demands many sacrifices; Thérèse's reply and face showed she was ready (Process of the Ordinary p.211). In her cell she said, "Now I am here for ever."
- When / where: 9 Apr 1888; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Louis; Léonie; Céline; Marie; Pauline
- Source: LAV p.195–196

- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high
### F-LF-188 — Delatroëtte's "Te Deum" speech at the enclosure door
- Claim: Canon Delatroëtte, ecclesiastical superior, stood with Louis present and the enclosure door open, and said coldly: "Well, my Reverend Mothers, you can now chant a Te Deum. As the Bishop's delegate, I present to you this child of fifteen whose entrance you have desired. I trust she may not disappoint your hopes, but I remind you that if it should turn out otherwise, the responsibility will be yours alone." The sisters were "chilled".
- When / where: 9 Apr 1888; the enclosure door
- People: Delatroëtte; Louis; Mère Marie de Gonzague; the community

- Notes: Opponent type: superior; reason: her youth. Louis heard it.
### F-LF-189 — How the community received the postulant
- Claim: The novice mistress, Mère Marie des Anges, said Thérèse "surprised the community by her bearing… marked by a certain majesty that one would not expect in a child of fifteen" (Apostolic Process p.184). Mère Agnès said the sisters expected a child, and were met by "dignified yet modest bearing". Sr Saint-Jean de la Croix had opposed the entry of so young a postulant, and later told Mère Agnès, "I said to myself, 'They will both be disappointed.' How deceived I was. Sœur Thérèse… is extraordinary" (Summarium 1914 p.679).
- When / where: Apr 1888 and later; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Mère Marie des Anges; Mère Agnès (Pauline); Sr Saint-Jean de la Croix; Thérèse
- Source: LAV p.197
- Source type: eyewitness
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high for testimony as printed
- Notes: Opponent type: community member; reason: precedent and youth.
### F-LF-190 — The rule she lived by
- Claim: Laveille sums up the Carmelite rule: Breviary office; the word of God "six and often seven hours a day" (office, reading, meditation); continual fast from 14 Sep to Easter plus Church fasts and every Friday of the year; perpetual abstinence from meat except in sickness; profound silence except two hours' recreation daily; work in cell or office; coarse serge; a bed of "a hard palliasse supported on three planks"; never to excuse oneself even if unjustly accused; complete obedience. Only the community room was heated. Thérèse admitted in last illness that she had suffered from cold "even to death" (a sister: she walked about fifty yards through open cloisters and an icy corridor to her cell, slept in two thin blankets, and shivered all night, never asking for relief).
- When / where: 1888–1897; Lisieux Carmel
- People: the community; Thérèse
- Source: LAV p.197–198, p.309–310
- Source type: early-life / eyewitness
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Sensory detail: alpargates (hemp sandals), chilblains (LAV p.236, p.259).
### F-LF-192 — The cobweb, the weeding, "continually complaining"
- Claim: Thérèse wrote that Marie de Gonzague treated her severely and she "could never meet her without receiving some reproof" (HA ch. vii p.117). She left a cobweb in the cloister and the Prioress said before the community, "It can be easily seen that our cloisters have been swept by a child of fifteen. Too bad! Go, sweep away that cobweb…" In the rare hours of direction Thérèse was "reprimanded nearly all the time". As postulant she was sent at 4.30 p.m. to weed the garden, and often met the Prioress, who said, "After all, this child does absolutely nothing. What sort of novice would she be who must be sent out every day for a walk?" Thérèse also plied her needle in her free time, without raising her eyes, because she thought the Prioress wanted it. Told to report every sickness, she did so daily "through obedience"; the Prioress said, "This child is continually complaining… If she cannot bear her pains and aches, her place is not amongst us."
- When / where: 1888–1890; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Marie de Gonzague; Mère Marie des Anges; Thérèse
- Source: LAV p.200, p.316–317 (HA ch. vii p.117; Summarium 1919 p.518 §1393)
- Source type: own-writing / eyewitness
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes (baseline names Marie de Gonzague only)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Health detail: the "attacks of pain" of the postulant are not named. Surprise: postulant reported illness daily and risked dismissal.
### F-LF-194 — Mère Marie des Anges, and the sleep she forgot
- Claim: Mère Marie des Anges (of the de Chaumontel family; died 1924 aged 79) knew Thérèse from age nine in the parlour. She said Thérèse grew "in grace and wisdom" from entry and a "venerable" nun later said, "we had never seen anything like it". Laveille says the mistress, not grasping Thérèse's interior silence, gave "long exhortations" in early months that hindered her prayer. Thérèse told her of the Prioress's severity "without the least murmur". The novice, weakened by austerity, was allowed extra sleep, but the mistress forgot, then made her rest fifteen days running; the Prioress, not seeing her at morning meditation, blamed her, and Thérèse "did not then know whom to obey". On the eve of profession she doubted her vocation, and the mistress "laughed heartily and completely reassured" her (see F-LF-203).
- When / where: 1888–1890; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Mère Marie des Anges; Marie de Gonzague; Thérèse
- Source: LAV p.201–203 (Apostolic Process p.215; Process of the Ordinary p.665)
- Source type: eyewitness
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
### F-LF-195 — Spiritual direction: Youf, Blino, Pichon
- Claim: The chaplain, Abbé Youf, had true piety but poor health; "it was impossible to obtain from him direction properly so called". He favoured her wish for daily Communion "as far as discretion permitted". Fr Blino SJ, when Thérèse said, "Father, I want to become a saint… as much as St Teresa did", replied "What pride and presumption! Confine yourself to the correction of your faults" (Mère Agnès, Summarium 1919 p.248). Fr Almire Pichon SJ (born at Carrouges near Alençon; 1,015 retreats by 1915; died 1919) had been the family's director. He preached the retreat at the Carmel in 1888. Thérèse was in aridity and asked whether she was loved or hated. At her general confession he said, "In the presence of God, of the Blessed Virgin, of all the Angels and Saints, I declare that you have never committed a single mortal sin." His superiors then sent him to Canada and she had one letter a year (HA ch. vii p.119). Pichon later testified, "I do not think that I ever had… to warn her against illusion… the good God willed to make her a great saint" (Summarium 1919 p.362, p.364).
- When / where: 1888–1890s; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Abbé Youf; Fr Blino SJ; Fr Almire Pichon SJ; Thérèse
- Source: LAV p.203–206
- Source type: eyewitness / own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: partial (baseline names Pichon)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Laveille dates the retreat to two months after entry (about June 1888) per Thérèse's own wording.
### F-LF-196 — Louis's letter after her entry, and his self-offering
- Claim: On 10 Apr 1888, to Nogrix, Louis wrote: "Thérèse, ma petite reine, est entrée hier au Carmel !… Dieu seul peut exiger un tel sacrifice… Ne me plaignez pas, car mon cœur surabonde de joie." Laveille says that Louis then came to the Carmel and told his three daughters that at Notre-Dame in Alençon he had received such graces that he prayed, "My God, it is too much… I want to suffer something for Thee", and offered himself; he did not finish, and the daughters understood him as offering himself as a victim. Laveille adds that "shortly afterwards, a second attack of paralysis seemed to indicate that the holocaust had been accepted."
- When / where: 10 Apr 1888 and later 1888; Lisieux; Alençon
- People: Louis; François-Julien Nogrix; Thérèse; Pauline; Marie
- Source: Louis to Nogrix, 10 Apr 1888 (Carmel archive, family-letters notes); LAV p.213–214
- Source type: own-writing (Louis)
- Rights: unknown (transcription); public-domain (LAV)
- Translation: Thérèse, my little queen, entered Carmel yesterday! Only God can ask such a sacrifice… Do not pity me, for my heart overflows with joy.
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high for the letter; medium for the self-offering (no source or date in Laveille).
- Notes: The date of the Alençon prayer is not found (gap F-LF-266).
### F-LF-197 — Louis vanishes: the flight to Le Havre, 23–27 June 1888
- Claim: Louis left Les Buissonnets on Saturday morning 23 Jun 1888 after a crisis of mental confusion and told no one. On Sunday 24 Jun a letter came from the Le Havre post office asking for money. Isidore Guérin, Céline and Ernest Maudelonde went and found him at the Le Havre post office on Wednesday 27 Jun, and brought him home. Mme Guérin wrote on 26 Jun: "Que la Croix est lourde !" He had shaved off his beard and spoke of wanting solitude. At about 5 a.m. on 26 Jun a house next to Les Buissonnets caught fire while Léonie was alone there with the maid.
- When / where: 23–27 Jun 1888; Lisieux; Le Havre
- People: Louis; Isidore and Céline Guérin; Céline; Ernest Maudelonde; Léonie
- Source: Carmel place page https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/lieux/le-havre/ ; Mme Guérin's letters of 26 Jun 1888 (Carmel archive); O'Riordan blog https://leoniemartin.org/my-blog-about-st-therese/2013/6/25/louis-martin-is-found-at-le-havre-june-27-1888-125-years-ago.html (facts only, citing Clarke and Gaucher)
- Source type: eyewitness (letters) / scholarship
- Rights: unknown / facts-only
- Other sources: Céline 1909 refers obliquely to a wish to "flee into a desert".
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high
- Notes: Laveille omits it. The letter from Trouville in Mme Guérin's letter may relate to another stage (not read). Sr Agnès wrote on 17 Jun 1888 that Thérèse would play St Agnes on 21 Jun for the Prioress's feast (Carmel letter).
### F-LF-208 — Her offices: refectory, sacristy, oratory fresco, portress
- Claim: Thérèse told Mère Agnès on 13 Jul 1897: "On my entrance, I was put in the linen-room… I had also a staircase and dormitory to sweep… every evening at half-past four to weed the garden… After my reception, I was sent to the refectory until I was eighteen… putting the water and beer on the tables. After the Forty Hours celebration, in 1891, I was assigned to the Sacristy. From the end of June in the following year [1892], I was for two months without any office. It was during this time that I painted the fresco around the tabernacle in the oratory, and was third in the portress' office… then portress, and still to continue painting… until the elections of 1896, after which I returned to the Sacristy… I asked to be allowed to help Sister X with the mending." She loved to see her reflection at the bottom of the golden ciborium. Once she found a tiny particle of the Host on the paten, called companions to adore it and left it for the priest.
- When / where: 1888–1897; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Mère Agnès; Marie des Anges
- Source: LAV p.239 fn., p.239–240 (her words to Mère Agnès, 13 Jul 1897)
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Laveille adds that as refectorian she also distributed bread. Her paintings and poems: see works.md.
### F-LF-211 — The rue Labbey house, Le Juif, and La Musse summers
- Claim: In July 1892 Céline and Léonie rented 7 rue Labbey, Lisieux, where they lived with him until his death. The Le Juif family entered the Guérins' service in 1892; Désiré Le Juif (1861–1916) later served Céline, and was converted in 1893 before her painting of the Virgin and Mary Magdalene after a novena to St Joseph. Each summer from 1888 (Carmel: 1888–1898) the Martins and Guérins spent some months at the Château de la Musse near Évreux (Eure), which the Guérins inherited in 1888 from Auguste David, a first cousin of Céline Guérin's mother (Fortin) . Thérèse never went. Laveille says she found it hard to keep worldly influence from "the hospitable and beautiful château".
- When / where: 1888–1898; Lisieux; La Musse
- People: Céline; Léonie; Louis; the Guérins; Désiré Le Juif
- Source: Carmel place pages https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/lieux/la-musse/ , https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/lieux/rues-labbey-et-paul-banaston/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/personnage/le-juif-desire/ ; https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/monsieur-guerin/ ; LAV p.249
- Source type: scholarship
- Rights: unknown
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high
- Notes: The Guérins sold La Musse on 11 May 1898 (Carmel place page) or in 1899 (Fortin). Conflict logged in gaps file. Mme Guérin's inheritance: "from the Fournet side as such" is not correct per the place page.
### F-LF-240 — What she looked like, and the objects she used
- Claim: Laveille prints a description "from contemporary information": "She was tall of stature and slight, with golden hair and grey-blue eyes, eyebrows very slightly arched, a small mouth, refined and regular features. Her face, fair as a lily, was well proportioned…" (Appendix to HA, p.596). Her life-long lamp had a wick that could only be raised with a pin. She gave Céline her good inkstand and holy-water font on Céline's entry and kept old ones from the garret. She used worn pens, wrote her manuscript in "two series of miserable exercise-books", scribbled poems on old envelopes and gave them away. She carried a small Gospel volume near her heart until death and once, in temptations against faith, wrote the Credo in her blood in it, as one confessor counselled. She kept the picture of the Holy Face in her Breviary. Her hands in winter were "swollen and covered with painful chilblains". Her hearing was "extremely keen" (she was bothered by a sister rattling beads).
- When / where: 1888–1897; Lisieux Carmel
- People: Thérèse; Céline
- Source: LAV p.208, p.232, p.236, p.249, p.259, p.281, p.296, p.310
- Source type: early-life / eyewitness
- Rights: public-domain
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: medium-high
- Notes: The descriptive source is anonymous ("contemporary information"). Photographs: F-LF-220. Céline's later adjustments of photos: not in my sources.

### F-OM-007 — Official and papal texts on the opposition to her entry into Carmel
- Claim: (a) The brief says her father, though he had already given three daughters to God and felt the parting bitterly, did not refuse; but the Church authorities flatly refused to let a girl of barely fifteen take up so strict a life; she went to Rome with her father's pilgrimage, knelt before Leo XIII, who sent her back to her superiors; she bore this calmly until the bishop, "moved by such virtue", gave permission. (b) Benedict XV, in his 1921 allocution, adds that her uncle would not consent until she was seventeen, that the ecclesiastical superior of the Carmel wanted to wait until she was twenty-one, and that after the bishop agreed the prioress still delayed her entry four months; she complained against none of them.
- When / where: 1887–1888
- People: Louis Martin; Isidore Guérin (the "brother-in-law"); Bishop Hugonin of Bayeux; the ecclesiastical superior of the Carmel (Canon Delatroëtte, not named in these texts); the prioress (Marie de Gonzague, not named); Leo XIII
- Source: (a) AAS 15 p. 204; (b) Taylor 1926/30 PDF 290–291 (Benedict XV allocution, English)
- Source type: official | Rights: public-domain
- Original words (a): « ecclesiastici moderatores puellam vix quindecim annos natam se rigidissimae religiosae vitae posse mancipare omnino negarunt »
- Translation: "the Church authorities utterly refused that a girl barely fifteen should bind herself to the very strict religious life"
- Other sources: the bull (AAS 17 p. 339) says she met "many difficulties from the Church authorities" and that Leo XIII sent her back to her superiors.
- New to English readers: partial (baseline has the Rome audience; the pope's own list of objectors, with the ages 17 and 21 and the four-month delay, is new)
- Confidence: high
- Notes: Reactions and opposition. Names in brackets are not in these sources.
### F-OM-008 — The novice mistress said she never had so devout a pupil

- When / where: 1888, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: the novice mistress (Marie des Anges, not named here)

### F-OW-060 — Blood from the hand of Christ and "I thirst"
- Claim: One Sunday, looking at a picture of Christ on the Cross, she was struck by blood falling from one of his hands with no one to gather it; she resolved to stand in spirit at the foot of the Cross to receive it and pour it on souls. Christ's cry "I thirst!" rang in her heart; "I felt myself devoured by the thirst for souls", first for "great sinners".
- When / where: 1887 (a Sunday; she does not give the date), Lisieux
- People: —
- Source: Ms A 45v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « je me sentais moi-même dévorée de la soif des âmes »
- Translation: "I felt myself eaten up with thirst for souls."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-061 — Study of history and science; the Imitation in her muff; Arminjon
- Claim: After Christmas 1886 she felt "an extreme desire to know" and studied history and science alone, limiting herself to a set number of hours. She knew almost all of the Imitation of Christ by heart; she carried it in her pocket in summer and in her muff in winter; at her aunt's they opened it at random and made her recite the chapter. At 14 she read Abbé Arminjon's conferences "on the end of the present world and the mysteries of the future life", lent to her father by the Carmelites; she read it at the window of her study room and copied passages on perfect love.
- When / where: 1887, Les Buissonnets
- People: Guérin family; Abbé Charles Arminjon (author)
- Source: Ms A 46v–47v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « en été, je le portais dans ma poche, en hiver, dans mon manchon »
- Translation: "in summer I carried it in my pocket, in winter in my muff"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-062 — Evening talks with Céline in the belvedere
- Claim: After Christmas the age gap disappeared; before, Céline had said Thérèse would have to grow "the height of a stool" to share her secrets, and Thérèse used to stand on the stool. Each evening in the belvedere they watched the moon rise behind the trees and talked of Heaven; she compares it to Monica and Augustine at Ostia. Her confessor allowed Communion four times a week in May, and a fifth on feasts; she did not ask, but later writes a soul "should tell her confessor the attraction she feels to receive her God".
- When / where: 1887, Les Buissonnets
- People: Céline; her confessor (Abbé Lepelletier, not named here)
- Source: Ms A 47v–48v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « il me faudrait grandir «de la hauteur d'un tabouret» »
- Translation: "I would have to grow 'the height of a stool'"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-063 — Pentecost 1887: telling her father; the little white flower
- Claim: After Vespers on Pentecost she found her father sitting by the cistern, hands joined, in the sunset. She sat beside him, eyes wet; he pressed her head to his heart: "What is it, my little queen? Tell me." Walking slowly with her head on his heart, she told him. His tears mixed with hers; he said only that she was very young; she pleaded so well that he agreed and said God did him a great honour to ask for his children. He picked a little white flower "like a lily in miniature" from a low wall, roots and all, and explained how God had made it grow. She put it in her Imitation at the chapter "That we must love Jesus above all things"; "it is still there, only the stem has broken near the root".
- When / where: 29 May 1887, garden of Les Buissonnets
- People: Louis Martin
- Source: Ms A 50r–50v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Qu'as-tu ma petite reine?... confie-moi cela... »
- Translation: "What is it, my little queen? Tell me about it."
- Other sources: In the 1911 text (ch. V). The flower is preserved at the Carmel (check other parts).
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-064 — Pranzini: "my first child"
- Claim: She heard of a great criminal condemned to death who seemed likely to die impenitent. She offered "all the infinite merits of Our Lord" and asked Céline to have a Mass said for her intentions, not daring to ask herself for fear of having to admit it was for Pranzini; Céline got the secret out of her and joined her. She told God she was sure he would forgive Pranzini even without confession, but asked for "a sign" for her own consolation. Despite her father's ban on reading newspapers, she read about Pranzini; the day after the execution she opened La Croix: Pranzini had not confessed, but on the scaffold he turned, took the crucifix the priest held out, and kissed its wounds three times. She hid to cry. She calls him "my first child".
- When / where: summer 1887 (execution 31 Aug 1887, Paris; news read 1 Sep 1887), Lisieux
- People: Céline; Henri Pranzini; the prison chaplain (unnamed)
- Source: Ms A 45v–46v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « les lèvres de «mon premier enfant» allèrent se coller sur les plaies sacrées !!! »
- Translation: "the lips of 'my first child' pressed themselves to the sacred wounds!"
- Other sources: In the 1911 text (ch. V). Execution date is from other sources.
- New to English readers: no for the event; partial for the father's newspaper ban and the Mass via Céline.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: She disobeyed (or thought she did not disobey) her father's ban on newspapers: « Malgré la défense que Papa nous avait faite de lire aucun journal, je ne croyais pas désobéir ».
### F-OW-065 — Her uncle's refusal and three days of darkness
- Claim: Her uncle forbade her to speak of her vocation before 17: letting a 15-year-old enter Carmel was against "human prudence"; Carmelite life looked in the world's eyes like "a philosopher's life"; "everyone would talk"; it would take a miracle. After some time she endured "a very painful martyrdom that lasted three days", "the deep night of the soul"; it rained in torrents all three days. On the fourth day, a Saturday, her uncle called her into his study, reproached her gently for seeming afraid of him, and said he had only asked God for "a simple inclination of the heart"; she was "a little flower God wanted to pick" and he would no longer oppose it.
- When / where: Oct 1887, Lisieux
- People: Isidore Guérin
- Source: Ms A 50v–51v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « cette vie de carmélite étant aux yeux du monde une vie de philosophe »
- Translation: "since Carmelite life looks to the world like a philosopher's life"
- Other sources: Her letters of Oct 1887 (LT) give dates; see letters cards.
- New to English readers: yes (uncle's reasons not in baseline)
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: She notes: "in all the grave circumstances of my life, nature was the image of my soul".
### F-OW-066 — The superior of the Carmel refuses: "not before 21"
- Claim: The Carmel's superior (Canon Delatroëtte, not named in the text) would not allow her entry before 21. She went with her father and Céline to see him; he received them "very coldly": there was "no danger in delay", she could live a Carmelite life at home, "if I did not take the discipline all would not be lost"; he was only the bishop's delegate. She left the presbytery in tears under her umbrella in pouring rain and said she would go "to the Holy Father" if the bishop refused.
- When / where: Oct 1887, presbytery of Saint-Jacques, Lisieux
- People: Canon Jean-Baptiste Delatroëtte (superior; unnamed here); Louis Martin; Céline
- Source: Ms A 51v–52r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « si je ne prenais pas la discipline tout ne serait pas perdu »
- Translation: "if I didn't take the discipline all would not be lost"
- New to English readers: yes
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-067 — Willing to be in hell so that God is loved there
- Claim: One evening, not knowing how to tell Jesus she loved him, she thought with sorrow that he could never receive an act of love from hell, "so I told God that to please him I would gladly consent to be plunged there, so that he would be loved eternally in that place of blasphemy". "When one loves, one feels the need to say a thousand follies."
- When / where: autumn 1887, Les Buissonnets
- People: —
- Source: Ms A 52r–52v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain

- Translation: "when you love, you feel the need to say a thousand foolish things"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-068 — Minding two motherless little girls
- Claim: A relative of their maid died young leaving three small children; during her illness the family took in the two girls (the elder not yet six). Thérèse looked after them all day; to make them give way to each other she promised not toys but Heaven's rewards; the elder called her "la grande demoiselle".
- When / where: 1887, Les Buissonnets
- People: two unnamed little girls, relatives of the maid
- Source: Ms A 52v

- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-069 — Bayeux, 31 Oct 1887: hair put up, and the bishop
- Claim: It poured. Her father sent her by omnibus to the cathedral so her fine dress would not be soaked; there was a big funeral and everyone in black stared at her "light dress and white hat"; her father, "with patriarchal simplicity", made her climb to the top of the cathedral. Abbé Révérony was out; they wandered the streets; at a fine hotel she ate little. Révérony, seeing tears: "Ah, I see diamonds... you mustn't show them to Monseigneur!" In the bishop's study there were three huge armchairs; Révérony made her sit in the middle one "where four like me would have fitted". She spoke for herself. Révérony laughed: "you can hardly say you've wanted this for 15 years." The bishop said he must first speak with the Carmel's superior; she cried; Mgr Hugonin put her head on his shoulder and caressed her. Her father told him she had put her hair up to look older; "Monseigneur never speaks of 'his little girl' without telling the story of the hair". Her father asked Révérony, turning around: "Am I dressed well enough like this?" for the Pope.
- When / where: 31 Oct 1887, Bayeux
- People: Mgr Flavien Hugonin (bishop of Bayeux); Abbé Maurice Révérony (vicar general); Louis Martin
- Source: Ms A 53v–55r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Ah! je vois des diamants... il ne faut pas les montrer à Monseigneur!... »
- Translation: "Ah, I see diamonds... you mustn't show them to Monseigneur!"
- New to English readers: partial (baseline lists "visit to Bishop Hugonin" only)
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-070 — Notre-Dame des Victoires, Paris: peace about the smile returns
- Claim: Leaving Lisieux at 3 a.m., her father sang in the train "Roule, roule, ma diligence". In Paris the only wonder that "ravished" her was Notre-Dame des Victoires: "the Blessed Virgin made me feel it was really she who had smiled at me and cured me"; from then on she could call her only "Maman". She also asked there to be kept from anything that could tarnish her purity on the trip. They consecrated themselves to the Sacred Heart at Montmartre and left Paris on 7 Nov.

- People: Louis Martin; Céline

- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-071 — On pilgrimage: her shyness vanished; priests are "weak and fragile men"
- Claim: On the pilgrimage (mostly nobility) she found herself "completely free" of her shyness and spoke easily with great ladies, priests and the bishop of Coutances. Titles and "de"s seemed "only smoke". Living a month with "many holy priests" she saw that "they are nonetheless weak and fragile men"; this taught her the Carmel's purpose, to pray for priests: "I understood my vocation in Italy." She noticed Abbé Révérony watching her at table, leaning over to see her and hear what she said.
- When / where: 7 Nov – 2 Dec 1887, Switzerland and Italy
- People: Céline; Louis Martin; Abbé Révérony; Mgr Germain (bishop of Coutances)

### F-OW-072 — Milan cathedral roof, the grumbling Frenchman, and the students of Bologna
- Claim: At Milan Céline and Thérèse climbed to the top of the marble spire while timid ladies hid their faces; they knelt with their heads on St Charles Borromeo's reliquary during the bishop's Mass. At the Campo Santo an old Frenchman said crossly, "Ah, how enthusiastic the French are!"; he complained of everything; her father offered him his seat. She found Venice "sad". Bologna became "unbearable" because of the students who lined the streets, "above all because of the little adventure that happened to me with one of them".

### F-OW-073 — Loreto: Communion inside the Holy House
- Claim: At Loreto, Communion was given only at one altar in the basilica, outside the Holy House. "It was in the diamond itself and not in the case that we wanted to receive." Her father did as everyone did, but Céline and Thérèse found a priest about to say Mass inside the Santa Casa by special privilege; he put two small hosts on his paten for them. She also put her rosary in the Child Jesus' little bowl, and speaks of "furtively scratching the walls" to take a souvenir.
- When / where: Nov 1887, Loreto
- People: Céline; Louis Martin; an unnamed priest

- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-074 — Into the Colosseum over the barrier
- Claim: The arena was closed off by a barrier. She thought of asking a passing workman for his ladder but did not, "he would have taken me for a madwoman". Then she found a place where the rubble reached the top of the barrier and cried to Céline, "Come quick, we can get through!" They climbed over crumbling ruins while their father called them back; Céline found the cross-marked stone where martyrs fought; they knelt and Thérèse kissed the dust and asked to be a martyr. They took some stones. Their father "could not scold us" and was proud of their courage.
- When / where: Nov 1887, Rome
- People: Céline; Louis Martin
- Source: Ms A 60v–61r

- New to English readers: partial

### F-OW-075 — St Cecilia, the catacombs, and a piece of mosaic for St Agnes
- Claim: In the catacombs they slipped away to the bottom of St Cecilia's old tomb and took earth from it; Cecilia became "my favourite saint, my intimate confidante". At St Agnes they could not get a relic for Pauline (Sr Agnès) except a small red stone that fell from an old mosaic.
- When / where: Nov 1887, Rome
- People: Céline
- Source: Ms A 61r–62r

- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-076 — The audience with Leo XIII, 20 Nov 1887
- Claim: Dressed in black with lace mantillas and a Leo XIII medal on a blue and white ribbon, they attended the Pope's 8 a.m. Mass. At the audience each pilgrim knelt, kissed the Pope's foot and hand; two noble guards touched them to rise. Révérony, at the Pope's right, sent word that no one was to speak to the Pope. Thérèse looked at Céline: "Speak!" Instead of kissing his hand she joined hers: "Most Holy Father, in honour of your jubilee, allow me to enter Carmel at 15!" The Pope said, "I don't understand very well"; Révérony explained. The Pope: "Well, my child, do what the superiors tell you." With her hands on his knees: "Oh Holy Father, if you said yes, everyone would agree!" He looked at her and said, stressing each syllable, "Go... go... You will enter if God wills it!" The guards and Révérony lifted her by the arms and carried her almost to the door; the Pope put his hand on her lips and then blessed her. Céline asked a blessing for the Carmel; Révérony said crossly "The Carmel is already blessed"; the Pope repeated gently "Oh yes, it is already blessed." Earlier the Pope had put his hand on her father's head.
- When / where: Sunday 20 Nov 1887, Vatican
- People: Leo XIII; Abbé Révérony; Céline; Louis Martin; two noble guards
- Source: Ms A 62v–63v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « Allons... Allons... Vous entrerez si le Bon Dieu le veut!... »
- Translation: "Come, come... You will enter if God wills it!"
- Other sources: In the 1911 text (ch. VI). Céline's and Révérony's accounts in other parts.
- New to English readers: no for the event; partial for the physical details (carried out by guards, hand on her lips).
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-077 — "The little ball": after the audience
- Claim: She had offered herself to the Child Jesus as "his little plaything", a ball of no value he could throw, kick, pierce, leave in a corner or press to his heart. "In Rome Jesus pierced his little toy; he wanted to see what was inside, and having seen it... he let his little ball fall and went to sleep." That day it rained. A few days later at Brother Siméon's, Révérony was friendly; the old Brother said, "You don't see that in Italy!" On a train platform M. Legoux, vicar general of Coutances, asked, "Well, how is our little Carmelite?"; she learned the whole pilgrimage knew.
- When / where: Nov 1887, Rome and the return journey
- People: Brother Siméon (Christian Brother in Rome); Révérony; Abbé Legoux
- Source: Ms A 64r–65r
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain

- Translation: "In Rome Jesus pierced his little toy."
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-078 — Lost belt buckle at Assisi; a ride in the vicar general's carriage
- Claim: At Assisi, after the monastery of St Agnes, she lost her belt buckle; searching made her miss all the carriages except Révérony's, full of the most distinguished gentlemen. She asked for a seat; a gentleman gave his and sat by the driver. "I looked like a squirrel caught in a trap." She tried to tip the driver from her tiny purse; Révérony paid for both.
- When / where: Nov 1887, Assisi
- People: Abbé Révérony; unnamed gentlemen
- Source: Ms A 65v–66r

### F-OW-079 — Touching the nail of the Cross; women "excommunicated" in Italy
- Claim: At Santa Croce in Gerusalemme she slipped her little finger through an opening in the reliquary and touched one of the nails of the Cross: "I was really too bold!" In Florence only she could put her hand through the grille to touch rosaries to St Mary Magdalene de' Pazzi's tomb. She could not understand "why women are so easily excommunicated in Italy": they were told at every step "Don't go in here... don't go in there, you'd be excommunicated!" "Ah, poor women, how despised they are!" Yet women love God in greater numbers than men, and at the Passion they had more courage than the apostles. At a Carmelite friary she went into the inner cloister; an old friar waved her away; she pointed to the paintings to say they were pretty; he smiled and left.
- When / where: Nov–Dec 1887, Rome and Florence
- People: an unnamed old Carmelite friar
- Source: Ms A 66r–66v
- Source type: own-writing

- Translation: "Ah, the poor women, how they are looked down on!"

- New to English readers: yes (women passage cut); partial (nail)

### F-OW-080 — Waiting for the bishop's letter; Christmas 1887 without an answer
- Claim: Pauline told her to write to the bishop; her uncle found her letter too simple and rewrote it; Pauline then told her to wait; the letter went ten days before Christmas. Every morning after Mass she went to the post office with her father. Christmas came with no answer; "Jesus did not wake up". That afternoon at the Carmel grille she saw a little Jesus holding a ball with her name on it, and the nuns sang a hymn by Pauline. At home Céline had placed a little boat in a basin with the sleeping Jesus and a ball; on the sail "I sleep but my heart watches" and on the hull "Abandon!"

### F-OW-081 — New Year 1888: permission granted but delayed until after Lent
- Claim: A letter from Mère Marie de Gonzague told her the bishop's answer had come on 28 Dec but that she had decided the entry would be after Lent. She wept; "this time it was the holy ark that refused entry to the poor little dove". In the three months she decided to lead a serious, mortified life, but "I never did any penances... I always let myself be coddled in cotton wool and fattened like a little bird"; her mortifications were breaking her will, holding back a retort, doing small services unnoticed, not leaning her back when seated.
- When / where: 1 Jan – 9 Apr 1888, Lisieux
- People: Mère Marie de Gonzague; Mgr Hugonin

- Translation: "I always let myself be coddled in cotton wool and fattened up like a little bird."
- New to English readers: yes (the delay by the prioress and her self-mockery are not in baseline)
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-082 — Entry to Carmel, 9 Apr 1888
- Claim: The evening before, the family dined together; her father said almost nothing but looked at her; her aunt cried; cousin Marie asked her pardon for the pain she thought she had caused. On the morning she looked a last time at Les Buissonnets. At Mass everyone sobbed except her; her heart beat so violently she thought she might die. She knelt for her father's blessing; he knelt too and blessed her weeping. Inside, she was taken to the dark choir with the Blessed Sacrament exposed, and first saw "the eyes of our holy Mother Geneviève" fixed on her. She repeated, "It is for ever, for ever that I am here!"
- When / where: 9 Apr 1888 (feast of the Annunciation transferred), Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Louis Martin; the Guérins; Léonie; Céline; Mère Marie de Gonzague; Mère Geneviève de Sainte-Thérèse

- Translation: "to give it, he knelt down himself and blessed me, weeping"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-083 — Fr Pichon's verdict: "never a mortal sin"
- Claim: About two months after her entry, Fr Pichon came for Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur's profession. He had thought her fervour "quite childish and her way very gentle". She made a general confession with difficulty; he said: "In the presence of God, the Blessed Virgin and all the saints, I declare that you have never committed a single mortal sin." And: "if he abandoned you, instead of a little angel you would become a little demon." "My child, may Our Lord always be your Superior and your Novice Master." Soon after he left for Canada; she got one letter a year for the twelve she wrote him.
- When / where: May 1888, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: Fr Almire Pichon SJ; Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur
- Source: Ms A 70r; 71r

- Rights: public-domain

- Translation: "I declare that you have never committed a single mortal sin."
- New to English readers: partial (Pichon is in Wikipedia; the words and the letter ratio are not)
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-084 — The prioress's severity; the "very simple soul"
- Claim: "Our Mother" (Marie de Gonzague), often ill, had little time for her; she loved Thérèse, "yet God allowed that, without her knowing it, she was very severe; I could not meet her without kissing the ground". Her novice mistress (Sr Marie des Anges) was "a real saint" with whom she worked all day, yet "my soul did not open". An old Mother told her at recreation: "My little girl, I don't think you can have much to tell your superiors... because your soul is extremely simple."
- When / where: 1888–1890, Carmel of Lisieux

- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-085 — Her father's first crisis, June 1888
- Claim: Her father had had "an attack of paralysis in the legs" in May 1887; on the Rome trip he tired easily. In June 1888, "and above all the 24th", came "our bitterness"; she will not write it, "these memories are too well engraved". His "second attack" delayed her clothing; he recovered and the bishop fixed 10 Jan 1889.

- People: Louis Martin
- Source: Ms A 71v–72r

### F-OW-089 — Novice: the lamp taken, the chipped jug, the broken vase
- Claim: As a postulant she liked having "nice things" for her use. One evening after Compline in the Great Silence her lamp had been taken by mistake; she was glad to be deprived "even of necessary things". She came to love "the ugliest and least convenient objects": she was happy when the pretty jug in her cell was replaced by "a big chipped jug". A vase broken behind a window: the novice mistress thought it was her fault; she said nothing, kissed the floor, and promised to be tidier: "my first victory". She loved folding the mantles sisters left behind. Her one mortification from the world, not leaning her back, was forbidden "because of my tendency to stoop".
- When / where: 1888–1890, Carmel of Lisieux
- People: novice mistress (Sr Marie des Anges)
- Source: Ms A 74r–74v
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: « une grosse cruche tout ébréchée »
- Translation: "a big jug all chipped"
- New to English readers: partial
- Confidence: high.
### F-OW-257 — Her jobs in the Carmel, in her own list (told 13 July 1897)
- Claim: On entering: the linen room with the subprioress Sr Marie des Anges, plus sweeping the stairs and dormitory; asking for mortifications in the refectory cost her; she pulled weeds at 4:30, which displeased the prioress. After clothing (Jan 1889): the refectory until 18 (sweeping, putting out water and beer). Forty Hours 1891: sacristy with Sr Saint-Stanislas. From June 1892: two months without office (painted the angels of the oratory; tierce for the bursar). Then the turn (portress) with Sr Saint-Raphaël, keeping painting, until the elections of 1896, when she asked to help Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph in the linen room. People thought her slow and not devoted; Mère Agnès scolded her for a refectory tablecloth left unmended, "and I was wrong, it was time she lacked"; Thérèse cried without excusing herself. She hated spiders but cleaned the cobwebs from the dark hole of St Alexis under the stairs. Also (12 Jul): a violent inner struggle "with thoughts against authority" when told to prepare a night-light for Mère Marie de Gonzague's family, lodging unexpectedly with the turn sisters; she pretended it was for the Virgin and Child and made it spotless.
- When / where: 1888–1896, told 12–13 Jul 1897
- People: Sr Marie des Anges; Sr Saint-Stanislas; Sr Saint-Raphaël; Sr Marie de Saint-Joseph; Mère Marie de Gonzague; Mère Agnès (recorder)
- Source: CJ 12.7.1; 13.7.18, cj-juillet-1897
- Source type: eyewitness record of her words (Mère Agnès)
- Rights: public-domain; transcription unknown
- Original words: « il me venait de telles pensées contre l'autorité »
- Translation: "such thoughts against authority came to me"
- New to English readers: yes (her own job list and the night-light struggle)
- Confidence: medium-high.
- Notes: Central for daily life in Carmel.
### F-OW-347 — An April Fool to Céline
- Claim: On the eve of 1 April, Thérèse wrote Céline a two-line mock-solemn rhyme about her "Diadème" passing to Céline's head, then "Poisson d'Avril!" and the promise of a comb from the April fish.
- When / where: 31 March 1887, Les Buissonnets.
- People: Céline.
- Source: LT 22 (to Céline, 31 Mar 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-22-a-celine-31-mars-1887/

- Original words: « Je garde mon Diadème jusqu'à Demain matin / Mais après sur ta tête passera mon Destin / Poisson d'Avril !... / Demain tu auras un peigne que le poisson d'avril te donnera. »
- Translation: "I keep my Diadem until tomorrow morning, but after that my Destiny will pass onto your head. April Fool! Tomorrow you will get a comb that the April fish will give you."
- New to English readers: yes.
- Confidence: high for text; low for what the "diadème" refers to (not explained).
- Notes: Playfulness three months after the Christmas 1886 "conversion".
### F-OW-348 — "Louploup": teasing her sick cousin
- Claim: Her cousin Marie Guérin had a toothache. Thérèse writes a playful note: she asks herself the questions and answers them "to my advantage". She had just come back from visiting the Carmel, where Marie and Pauline will pray for Marie Guérin. She calls her cousin "mon petit Louploup" and says the Louploup must deserve its name and eat like a real wolf. She notes her letter is crooked because she has not held a pen for so long.
- When / where: Monday 27 June 1887, Les Buissonnets; the Guérins at Trouville.
- People: Marie Guérin; Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur and Sr Agnès (in the Carmel).

- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Il faut que mon petit Louploup mérite son nom, qu'il mange comme un vrai Louploup. » ; « Je te quitte en t'embrassant non pas sur les deux joues, je craindrais de te faire mal aux dents, mais sur ton joli petit front. »
- Translation: "My little Louploup must deserve its name and eat like a real wolf." / "I leave you with a kiss, not on both cheeks, for fear of hurting your teeth, but on your pretty little forehead."

### F-OW-349 — "My sermons on death", "my eyes that fascinate you", and the silkworms
- Claim: To her cousin Jeanne, Thérèse drew a sailing boat herself because "the artist Darel" was not there. She jokes that Jeanne must be glad no longer to hear "my sermons on death", no longer to see "my eyes that fascinate you", and no longer to be pushed on the way to the Misses Pigeon. Eight of her silkworms have died; four remain. She blames Céline, who "gave them so much care" that they died "of grief or of sudden apoplexy". "The tall Englishwoman" has left. Papa will leave on Wednesday for a short stay in Alençon, so the aunt's invitation cannot be accepted.
- When / where: 27 June 1887, Les Buissonnets; Guérins at Trouville.
- People: Jeanne Guérin (cousin); "l'artiste Darel" (not identified); "la grande Anglaise" (not identified); "les demoiselles Pigeon" (not identified); Céline; Louis Martin; Mme Guérin.
- Source: LT 24 (to Jeanne Guérin, 27 Jun 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-24-a-jeanne-guerin-27-juin-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Je pense que tu es bien contente de ne plus entendre mes sermons sur la mort, de ne plus voir mes yeux qui te fascinent et de ne plus être poussée en allant chez les demoiselles Pigeon... / J'ai à vous annoncer la mort de huit de mes chers vers à soie [...] Céline leur a prodigué tant de soins qu'elle est arrivée à me les faire mourir presque tous de chagrin ou d'apoplexie foudroyante »
- Translation: "I think you are very glad no longer to hear my sermons on death, no longer to see my eyes that fascinate you, and no longer to be pushed on the way to the Misses Pigeon... I must announce the death of eight of my dear silkworms [...] Céline gave them so much care that she managed to make almost all of them die of grief or sudden apoplexy."

- Confidence: high.

### F-OW-350 — Léonie's last week at home before the Visitation; Céline's dead birds
- Claim: Léonie had two days left at the Buissonnets before entering the Visitation. Thérèse feels sorrow mixed with joy: she believes Léonie will be happy only there and will find at the Visitation "all that she lacks in the world". Céline is "in mourning" for her two cordon-bleu birds (finches); the male followed his mate the next morning, and their bodies are at the taxidermist. She teases Marie Guérin for her swollen cheeks: "why did you take your face to the sculptor again?"
- When / where: 14 July 1887, Les Buissonnets; Guérins at Trouville.
- People: Marie Guérin; Léonie Martin; Céline; Mme Guérin (ill); Jeanne Guérin; Louis Martin (Marie Guérin's godfather: "sa chère filleule").

- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « je suis heureuse de voir enfin ma chère Léonie dans son centre ; oui je crois que là seulement elle sera heureuse, à la Visitation elle trouvera tout ce qui lui manque dans le monde. / Céline est en deuil de ses deux petits cordons bleus [...] maintenant ses dépouilles mortelles sont chez l'empailleur. »
- Translation: "I am happy to see my dear Léonie at last in her element; yes, I believe only there will she be happy; at the Visitation she will find everything she lacks in the world. / Céline is in mourning for her two little cordon-bleus [...] now their mortal remains are at the taxidermist's."
- New to English readers: yes.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Léonie left the Visitation later (not covered in these letters until LT 29, when she is still there). Thérèse's hope "only there will she be happy" is a judgement about Léonie's difficulties at home; the letter does not name them.
### F-OW-351 — "I let my pen run like a little mad thing"
- Claim: Marie Guérin fell ill again on holiday. Thérèse scolds her playfully ("little wretch, as soon as you could have some fun, you hurry to be ill"). She pictured her as a "châtelaine" running in the park, but she lives as a patient in a gilded room that is only "a beautiful cage" for a bird. She admits she put "the plough before the oxen" in a sentence and asks Marie to follow the little crosses she added. She says her pen ran "like a little mad thing".
- When / where: 18 August 1887, Les Buissonnets.
- People: Marie Guérin; Isidore Guérin ("Mon oncle"); Mme Guérin; Jeanne Guérin.
- Source: LT 26 (to Marie Guérin, 18 Aug 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-26-a-marie-guerin-18-aout-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Tu es bien heureuse que je sois loin de toi, sans cela bien certainement tu aurais eu affaire à moi... [...] j'ai laissé courir ma plume comme une petite folle »
- Translation: "You are lucky I am far from you; otherwise you would certainly have had to deal with me... [...] I let my pen run like a little mad thing."
- New to English readers: yes.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Her cousin's poor health recurs through 1887–1890 (toothache, swollen cheeks, sore foot, influenza, and a "glande" before the Rome trip in LT 62).
### F-OW-352 — Her uncle's first answer: "a public scandal" — the world is the only obstacle
- Claim: Thérèse finally spoke to her uncle Isidore Guérin about entering Carmel. He was kind, gave her "a little very affectionate sermon", said he was very sure of her vocation, and that the only obstacle was "the world": it would be "a real public scandal" to see a child enter Carmel; she would be "the only one in all France". By the rules of human prudence she should not expect to enter before 17 or 18. If God wills it, He can show it. She did not mention any date. She quotes St Teresa: "God alone suffices."
- When / where: Saturday 8 October 1887, Lisieux (uncle's house). Written the same day.
- People: Isidore Guérin (uncle, pharmacist, her guardian figure after her mother's death); Pauline (Sr Agnès, recipient, "ma confidente"); Marie (Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, in retreat); Mère Marie de Gonzague.
- Source: LT 27 (to Sr Agnès de Jésus, 8 Oct 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-27-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-8-octobre-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « il m'a dit qu'il était très sûr de ma vocation, que ce n'était pas cela qui l'empêcherait de me laisser partir, il n'y a que le monde, je crois, qui est un obstacle. Ce serait un véritable scandale public que de voir entrer une enfant au Carmel, je serais la seule sur toute la France, etc... [...] il ne faut pas que je croie entrer avant dix-sept ou dix-huit ans »
- Translation: "He told me he was very sure of my vocation, that this was not what would stop him letting me go; I think only the world is an obstacle. It would be a real public scandal to see a child enter the Carmel; I would be the only one in all France, etc. [...] I must not think of entering before seventeen or eighteen."
- Other sources: Ms A (1911) tells the same talk but harsher: the uncle said it was against human prudence, that he would oppose it with all his power, and that only a miracle would change his mind. See the conflict card below.
- New to English readers: yes — "scandale public" ABSENT from 1911; the letter's gentler version is not in baseline.
- Confidence: high — contemporary letter written the same day.
- Notes: Type of conflict: family/social prudence. The uncle's stated reason is public opinion ("le monde"), not doubt about her vocation.
### F-OW-353 — Conflict: the uncle's refusal — letter of the same day vs Story of a Soul
- Claim: The two accounts of her uncle's first answer differ in tone. In LT 27 (written that day) he is "very good", "affectionate", "very sure" of her vocation, and names only "the world" and her age (17–18). In Ms A (written 1895, as printed 1911) he declares he will put "all possible opposition" in the way and will not change his mind "unless there is a miracle".
- When / where: 8 Oct 1887 (event); letter same day; Ms A 1895; 1911 print.
- People: Isidore Guérin; Thérèse; Pauline.
- Source: LT 27 (to Sr Agnès de Jésus, 8 Oct 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-27-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-8-octobre-1887/ ; compared with Histoire d'une âme 1911, chap. V (Gutenberg #36708).
- Source type: own-writing (both), different dates
- Rights: public-domain
- Original words: LT 27: « Mon oncle a été très bon [...] il m'a fait un petit sermon très AFFECTUEUX » ; 1911: « Il ajouta qu'il allait y mettre de son côté toute l'opposition possible, et qu'à moins d'un miracle, il ne changerait pas d'avis. »
- Translation: LT 27: "My uncle was very kind [...] he gave me a little, very AFFECTIONATE sermon." / 1911: "He added that for his part he would put up all possible opposition, and that unless there was a miracle he would not change his mind."
- New to English readers: yes — the letter's version is not in English baseline; Ms A's version is in Taylor.
- Confidence: medium — the letter tells Pauline she reports only the obstacles and that he said "many other very kind things"; she may have softened the scene for Pauline, or Ms A may have sharpened it. Both are her own words.
- Notes: LT 28 (23 Oct) confirms the uncle agreed "yesterday" (Saturday 22 Oct), which matches Ms A's "the fourth day, a Saturday".
### F-OW-354 — First letter to Fr Pichon: "I am not like my sisters"
- Claim: Thérèse asks Fr Almire Pichon SJ, director of her sisters, to take "the last one" as well. She says she cannot express herself well in a letter, unlike her sisters. She announces that "Papa is willing for me to enter at Christmas" and that her uncle, who found her too young, told her "yesterday" that he wanted to do God's will. Her sisters at the Carmel told her she could write to him "quite simply". She signs asking him to bless "your second little Lamb".
- When / where: 23 October 1887, Lisieux.
- People: Fr Almire Pichon SJ (Jesuit, director of Marie and Pauline); Louis Martin; Isidore Guérin; her sisters in the Carmel.
- Source: LT 28 (to Fr Pichon, 23 Oct 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-28-au-p-pichon-23-octobre-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « Je voudrais pouvoir me faire connaître à vous mais je ne suis pas comme mes sœurs, je ne sais pas bien dire dans une lettre tout ce que je ressens. [...] Papa veut bien que j'entre à Noël. [...] Bénissez votre second petit Agneau »
- Translation: "I would like to make myself known to you, but I am not like my sisters; I don't know how to say well in a letter all that I feel. [...] Papa is willing for me to enter at Christmas. [...] Bless your second little Lamb."
- Other sources: Fr Pichon is in the baseline by name only.
- New to English readers: yes — ABSENT from 1911 ("second petit Agneau", "entre à Noël").
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: Fixes the dates: father's consent before 23 Oct; uncle's consent 22 Oct 1887; target entry Christmas 1887. "Second little lamb": the letter does not say who the first was (perhaps Pauline, whom she elsewhere calls "l'agneau").
### F-OW-355 — To Léonie at the Visitation: kissing the Child Jesus's foot for her
- Claim: Léonie, then at the Visitation, had asked Thérèse to pray to Blessed Margaret Mary that she might become "a holy Visitandine"; Thérèse says she does so every day. Léonie had warned her to look after her pretty little statue of the Child Jesus; it is unharmed, and Thérèse "kissed his little foot" for Léonie. They are "so busy now" that Céline cannot write.
- When / where: 23–30 October 1887 (date uncertain in source), Lisieux; Léonie at the Visitation of Caen (the city is not named in the letter).
- People: Léonie Martin; Céline.
- Source: LT 29 (to Léonie, 23–30 Oct 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-29-a-leonie-23-30-octobre-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « il n'est pas abîmé, il est aussi frais que lorsque tu l'as quitté, j'ai embrassé son petit pied pour toi, sa petite main semblait te bénir de loin. »
- Translation: "It is not damaged; it is as fresh as when you left it. I kissed his little foot for you; his little hand seemed to bless you from afar."
- New to English readers: yes.
- Confidence: high; the letter is unfinished in the source (breaks off "mais").
- Notes: "Si pressées maintenant" — likely the preparations for the Rome pilgrimage (leaving early November); the letter does not say so.
### F-OW-356 — Paris, Hôtel de Mulhouse: exhausted, afraid of the carriages
- Claim: On the way to Rome, Thérèse writes from Paris that she is "extremely tired; everything is spinning around me". Paris has fine things "but all that is not happiness". Like her godmother Marie, she is always afraid of being run over; carriages surround her at every moment. She was very happy at Notre-Dame-des-Victoires and asked a grace for her cousin Jeanne at the Sacred Heart of Montmartre. Céline did not want her to write the day before.
- When / where: 6 November 1887, Paris, Hôtel de Mulhouse.
- People: Sr Agnès and Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur (recipients); Céline; Léonie; Jeanne Guérin; the Guérins; Mère Marie de Gonzague.
- Source: LT 30 (to Sr Agnès de Jésus and Sr Marie du Sacré-Cœur, 6 Nov 1887), https://archives.carmeldelisieux.fr/correspondance/lt-30-a-soeur-agnes-de-jesus-et-soeur-marie-du-sacre-coeur-6-novembre-1887/
- Source type: own-writing
- Rights: public-domain (her words); Carmel transcription/notes: unknown
- Original words: « je suis extrêmement fatiguée ; tout tourne autour de moi. Demain nous ne serons plus en France. [...] Je suis un peu comme ma chère Marraine, j'ai toujours peur d'être écrasée, à chaque instant je me trouve cernée par les voitures. »
- Translation: "I am extremely tired; everything is spinning around me. Tomorrow we will no longer be in France. [...] I am a bit like my dear Godmother; I am always afraid of being run over; at every moment I find myself hemmed in by carriages."
- Other sources: Ms A (1911) mentions Notre-Dame-des-Victoires and the Montmartre consecration.
- New to English readers: yes — "peur d'être écrasée" and "Hôtel de Mulhouse" ABSENT from 1911.
- Confidence: high.
- Notes: "Demain nous ne serons plus en France" does not match Ms A's route exactly (they left Paris 7 Nov and crossed Switzerland); it is her expectation at the time.
### F-OW-357 — Venice: "How small I felt before those gigantic mountains"; "Signorella"
- Claim: From Venice, Thérèse tells her cousin that crossing the Swiss mountains she felt small and that "one prays so well there; one feels God is there". That afternoon they visited Venice's monuments by gondola. The hotel staff call her "Signorella"; it is the only Italian word she understands. Céline makes her hurry and will not let her stay up late. She says the beauties of Italy cannot make her forget "my dear little Lisieux".
- When / where: Thursday 10 November 1887 (evening), Venice. A covering note (LT 31 B, Monday 14 Nov) says Céline forgot to post it for four days.
- People: Marie Guérin (recipient); Mme Guérin; Isidore Guérin; Jeanne Guérin; Céline; Louis Martin; Maria and Marcelline (named in a P.S.; Marcelline appears to be a Guérin servant).
